=====PDF PAGE 9=====
Preface
This book is addressed to researchers in mathematics or in physics eager to learn
the basics on vertex algebras and related geometrical aspects, such as arc spaces
and the concept of associated variety. It is based on several lecture courses given
by the authors in Pisa and Roma (Italy), Melbourne (Australia), Kyoto (Japan), Les
Diablerets (Switzerland), Dĳon (France), Rio de Janeiro (Brazil), Amherst (US),
Bengalore (India), etc.
Part I is devoted to the theory of arc spaces, the deﬁnition of vertex algebras, the
ﬁrst properties and basic examples of vertex algebras.
Part II is about Poisson vertex algebras, the notion of associated variety for vertex
algebras and Zhu’s functor, as well as the links between all these objects.
The BRST cohomology is the core of Part III. The W-algebras which are the
major motivations of the book are deﬁned via the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov re-
duction using the BRST cohomology. Their related algebraic/geometrical structures
introduced in Part III can also be deﬁned through the BRST cohomology: Slodowy
slices, ﬁnite W-algebras and arc spaces of Slodowy slices. All these structures are
studied in this part as well.
We focus in Part IV on quasi-lisse vertex algebras. These are vertex algebras
whose associated variety satisﬁes a certain ﬁniteness condition. They form a large
class of vertex algebras that enjoy remarkable properties similar to those sharing by
lisse or rational vertex algebras. Part IV includes various examples of such vertex
algebras, particularly in the context of W-algebras, and recent developments in the
area.
Each part contains exercises with hints collected at the end of the book. They are
ﬁve appendices, and an index.
The reader is supposed to be familiar with the basics in commutative algebra (for
instance, some spectral sequence arguments are used in Part III) and in representation
theory. Although there is an appendix giving a quick review on simple Lie algebras,
the reading of the book is greatly facilitated by a good knowledge on Lie theory.
7

=====PDF PAGE 10=====

=====PDF PAGE 11=====
Introduction
The goal of this book is to introduce the theory of vertex algebras and aﬃne W-
algebras, which are certain vertex algebras, with emphasis on their geometrical
aspects, in particular through the study of the arc spaces.
Vertex algebras are algebraic structures introduced by Richard Borcherds in 1986
[65] as a powerful tool in representation theory, motivated by the construction of an
inﬁnite-dimensional Lie algebra due to Igor Frenkel. They were notably used in the
monstrous moonshine conjecture (formulated by Conway and Norton), which sug-
gests an unexpected connection between the monster group and modular functions,
to build representations of sporadic groups. Vertex algebras also play a prominent
role in the representation theory of aﬃne Kac–Moddy Lie algebras.
In the meantime, they appear in string theory in physics. They give the rigorous
mathematical deﬁnition of the chiral part of a two-dimensional quantum ﬁeld the-
ory, intensively studied by Alexander Belavin, Alexander Polyakov and Alexander
Zamolodchikov [62].
After they were introduced by Borcherds, vertex algebras have turned out to be
extremely useful in many areas of mathematics, such as algebraic geometry (moduli
spaces), representation theory (modular representation theory, geometric Langlands
correspondence), combinatorics, two-dimensional conformal ﬁeld theory, string the-
ory (mirror symmetry) and four-dimensional gauge theory (AGT conjecture). In fact,
a recent discovery, which goes back to the works of Nakajima in 1994 [243], reveals
that vertex algebras appear in higher dimensional quantum ﬁeld theories as well, in
several ways. This gives new insights for the representation theory of vertex algebras.
Part I of the book is about the general theory of arc spaces, the deﬁnition of a
vertex algebras, the ﬁrst properties and examples.
Roughly speaking, a vertex algebra is a complex vector space 푉, endowed with a
distinguished vector, the vacuum vector, and the vertex operator map from 푉to the
space of formal Laurent series with linear operators on 푉as coeﬃcients. These data
satisfy a number of axioms and have some fundamental properties as, for example,
an analogue to the Jacobi identity, the locality and the associativity. The algebraic
law of a vertex algebra is governed by what is called the Operator Product Expansion
9

=====PDF PAGE 12=====
10
Introduction
(OPE). This notion and the precise deﬁnition of a vertex algebra are at the heart of
Chapter 2.
The simplest vertex algebras are the commutative ones (when the OPEs are
trivial). The latter are nothing but diﬀerential algebras, that is, unital commutative
algebras equipped with derivations. The jet algebras of unital commutative algebras
(of ﬁnite type) provide the typical examples of commutative vertex algebras. The
schemes corresponding to jet algebras, called the arc spaces, are of great importance
in the study of general vertex algebras. One of the reasons is that to any vertex algebra
is attached in a canonical way a certain Poisson variety whose arc space is closely
related to the graded vertex algebra. The geometry of the arc spaces arising in this
way reﬂects important properties of the vertex algebra. On another note, arc spaces
appear in the deﬁnition of the chiral de Rham complex on a complex manifold. The
study of jet algebras and arc spaces is of independent interest, and the book starts
with this topic (see Chapter 1).
Important basic examples of (noncommutative) vertex algebras are those arising
as representations of aﬃne Kac–Moody Lie algebras or Virasoro Lie algebras, [131,
179]. They are crucial in the representation theory of these Lie algebras, and that
of W-algebras, which are more sophisticated vertex algebras. We observe that many
vertex algebras that appear in the nature carry an action of the Virasoro algebra.
This leads to the notions of conformal vertex algebras and vertex operator algebras.
We refer to Chapter 3 for the study of these basic exemples, including the chiral
diﬀerential operators on an algebraic group (a particular case of chiral de Rham
complex).
Other important examples (not studied in this book) of noncommutative vertex
algebras are the lattice vertex algebras and the module moonshine, constructed by
Igor Frenkel, James Lepowsky, and Arne Meurman in 1988 [209].
Part II concerns several functors from the category of vertex algebras to the
categories of simpler objects (commutative and/or of ﬁnite type): Poisson algebras,
Poisson vertex algebras, associated algebras. These functors are extremely helpful
to study various aspects of the theory.
Vertex analogues of Poisson algebras are discussed in Chapter 4. In a summary
way, Poisson vertex algebras are diﬀerential algebras equipped with a Poisson vertex
휆-bracket {−휆−} compatible with the multiplication and the derivation. This theory
allows to study the integrability of partial diﬀerential equations eﬃciently, as it was
developed by Aliaa Barakat, Alberto De Sole, Victor Kac [50], among others. The
following are two examples of Poisson vertex algebras. First, taking the jet algebra
of a Poisson algebra gives a Poisson vertex algebras (cf. Section 4.2). Second, as
observed by Haisheng Li [214], any vertex algebra is ﬁltered and the corresponding
graded vertex algebra is naturally a Poisson vertex algebra (cf. Section 4.4). In fact,
to each vertex algebra 푉one can naturally attach, using the Zhu 퐶2-functor 푉7! 푅푉,
a certain Poisson variety 푋푉called the associated variety of 푉. The arc space of the
associated variety is deeply connected to the graded vertex algebra (cf. Section 4.5).
The associated variety is a fundamental geometrical invariant that captures important
properties of the vertex algebra.

=====PDF PAGE 13=====
Introduction
11
Another functor, studied in Chapter 5, is the Zhu functor 푉7! Zhu(푉). It attaches
to any vertex algebra an associative ﬁltered algebra which allows to parameterize the
simple modules of the vertex algebra in the best situation [282]. The graded Zhu’s
algebra of a vertex algebra 푉is a Poisson algebra deeply related to the associated
푋푉. Thus the knowledge of the associated variety is also important for the represen-
tation theory of vertex algebras. In fact, the associated varieties of vertex algebras,
especially in the context of aﬃne ones (those corresponding to aﬃne Kac–Moody
Lie algebras), play an analogue role to the associated varieties of primitive ideals of
the enveloping algebra of a simple Lie algebra. Figure 0.1 summarizes the diﬀerent
functors. We warn that the diagram is not commutative in general.

Fig. 0.1 Functors from the category of vertex algebras
From a given vertex algebra, there are several ways to construct new vertex
algebras: the coset construction is one of them, the BRST construction is another
one. The BRST construction comes up in several places. In particular it is used to
deﬁne the W-algebras, an important class of sophisticated vertex algebras associated
with nilpotent elements of simple Lie algebras.
The appearance of the AGT conjecture in physics led many researchers to-
wards these algebraic structures. The W-algebras were introduced and developed
by Zamolodchikov, Fateev–Lukyanov, Feigin–Frenkel and Kac–Roan–Wakimoto,
[62, 113, 117, 182] while their ﬁnite-dimensional analogues, the ﬁnite W-algebras
introduced by Premet [248, 249], have caught attention for diﬀerent reasons that are
more related to classical problems in representation theory. Thus W-algebras arise
in geometric representation theory and mathematical physics. They are related with
integrable systems, the two-dimensional conformal ﬁeld theory and the geometric
Langlands program.
The study of W-algebras is the major motivation of the book. They can be regarded
as aﬃnizations of ﬁnite W-algebras, and can also be considered as generalizations
of aﬃne Kac-Moody algebras and Virasoro algebras. They quantize the arc space of
the Slodowy slices associated with nilpotent elements.
Since they are not ﬁnitely generated by Lie algebras, the formalism of vertex
algebras is necessary to study them. The construction of aﬃne W-algebras is the
main purpose of Part III. In this context, associated varieties of W-algebras are
important tools related to the singularities of nilpotent Slodowy slices. In Part III is
introduced the BRST cohomology for the diﬀerent blocks of Figure 0.1, speciﬁcally:

=====PDF PAGE 14=====
12
Introduction
– Poisson algebras (coordinate rings of Slodowy slices, studied in Chapter 7),
– Associative algebras (ﬁnite W-algebras, studied in Chapter 8),
– Poisson vertex algebras (coordinate rings of the arc spaces of Slodowy slices,
studied in Chapter 9),
– Vertex algebras (aﬃne W-algebras, studied in Chapter 9 and Chapter 10)
The nicest vertex algebras are those which are both rational and lisse. The ratio-
nality means the complete reducibility of modules. The lisse condition means that
the associated variety has dimension zero. If a vertex algebra 푉is rational and lisse,
then it gives rise to a rational conformal ﬁeld theory. In particular, the characters
of simple 푉-modules form a vector-valued modular functions and, moreover, the
category of 푉-modules is a modular tensor category, so that one can associate with
it an invariant of knots.
Lisse (or 퐶2-coﬁnite) vertex algebras are natural generalizations of ﬁnite-
dimensional algebras. The modular invariance of characters still holds without the
rationality assumption. In fact the geometry of the associated variety often reﬂects
algebraic properties of the vertex algebra. Vertex algebras whose associated variety
has only ﬁnitely symplectic leaves are also of great interest for several reasons [42].
They were referred to as quasi-lisse vertex algebras by the ﬁrst author and Kazuya
Kawazetsu [32], and have been intensively studied since that time. Part IV is about
this family of vertex algebras. Examples of lisse and quasi-lisse vertex algebras are
presented in Chapter 11 and Chapter 12. Although the quasi-lisse condition is much
weaker than the ﬁniteness condition required for lisse or rational vertex algebras,
quasi-lisse vertex are still nice for many reasons that are explained in Chapter 13. In
particular their characters still satisfy certain modular invariant properties.
In the case where the vertex algebra is an aﬃne vertex algebra, its associated
variety is a conical subvariety of the corresponding simple Lie algebra, invariant by its
adjoint group. Such vertex algebras are quasi-lisse if and only if the associated variety
in contained in the nilpotent cone. For W-algebras, it happens when the associated
variety in contained in a nilpotent Slodowy slices. When the associated variety is
symplectic (which is the case of chiral diﬀerential operators) it loosely implies the
simplicity of the corresponding Poisson vertex algebras. All these observations are
exploited to study more advanced aspects of the representation theory of W-algebras,
such as the rationality question or the obtention of collapsing levels, see Chapter 14.
Associated varieties not only capture some of the important properties of vertex
algebras but also have interesting relationships with the Higgs branches of four-
dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theories (SCFTs) as discovered by Christo-
pher Beem and Leonardo Rastelli and coauthors [57]. For instance one can deduce
the modular invariance of Schur indices of 4D N = 2 SCFTs from the theory of
vertex algebras. On the other hand, Jethro Van Ekeren and Reimundo [102] Heluani
recently observed that the arc space of the associated variety is involved in the chiral
homology of elliptic curves with coeﬃcients in a quasiconformal vertex algebra. It
is believed by physicists that vertex algebras coming from 4D N = 2 SCFTs are
quasi-lisse. This implies a number of interesting conjectures and expectations that
are presented in Chapter 15 in connections with the 4D/2D duality in physics.

Introduction

Conventions and notation
Throughout the book, the ground ﬁeld will be the ﬁeld C of complex numbers unless
otherwise speciﬁed. We shall work with the Zariski topology, and by variety we
mean a reduced and separated scheme of ﬁnite type over C. We denote the smooth
part of a variety 푋by 푋reg and its complement by 푋sing. By a point of a scheme of
ﬁnite type over C we always mean a closed point. For a subscheme 푍of a scheme 푋,
the reduced subscheme of 푋with underlying space 푍is denoted by 푍red.

=====PDF PAGE 16=====

=====PDF PAGE 17=====
Part I
Vertex algebras: deﬁnitions and examples

=====PDF PAGE 18=====
This part aims to introduce the notion of vertex algebras. The heart of the mat-
ter is to give a deﬁnition. This is done in Chapter 2 after preliminary results on
formal series. We establish also the ﬁrst properties, and present basic examples (in
Chapter 3), essentially examples of vertex algebras generated by inﬁnite-dimensional
Lie algebras. Any ﬁnite-dimensional vertex algebra is commutative. Thus even the
smallest examples of noncommutative vertex algebras require signiﬁcant work.
Commutative vertex algebras are not, nevertheless, without signiﬁcance. The
structure sheaf of the arc spaces yields to interesting commutative vertex algebras.
In fact, are spaces not only provide examples of commutative vertex algebras, but
also serve as powerful tools in the study of general vertex algebras. We start the book
with their study in Chapter 1 from a slightly diﬀerent point of view than the usual
one, adopted for instance in the theory of singularities.

=====PDF PAGE 19=====
Chapter 1
Jet schemes and arc spaces
This chapter is devoted to the study of jet schemes and arc spaces associated with a
scheme 푋deﬁned over the ﬁeld of complex numbers.
Roughly speaking, an arc on a scheme 푋is a formal path on 푋, that is, a morphism
from the formal disc 퐷= Spec C[[푧]] to 푋. Jets on 푋are obtained by truncation of
such paths at a ﬁnite order.
The study of singularities via the space of arcs was initiated by Nash [245]. He
conjectured a close relationship between the geometry of the arc space and the sin-
gularities of 푋, see for instance Ishii and Kollár [169]. More precisely, he suggested
that studying the images under the truncation morphisms of the space of arcs should
provide information about the ﬁbers over the singular points in a resolution of sin-
gularities of 푋. The work of Mustaţă [242] supports these predictions; for instance,
rational singularities of a locally complete intersection variety can be detected by
the irreducibility of all its jet schemes. The space of arcs also plays a crucial role in
motivic integration, serving as the domain over which functions are integrated. We
direct the reader to the recent book by Chambert-Loir, Nicaise, and Sebag [84], as
well as the references provided therein, for further insights into this topic.
It turns out that arc spaces are also of great importance in the theory of vertex
algebras. One of the primary reasons is that the pushforward of the structure sheaf
of the arc scheme over a scheme 푋possesses the structure of a sheaf of commutative
vertex algebras over 푋([59, 122]), as detailed in Chapter 2. Furthermore, every vertex
algebra is canonically ﬁltered by the Li ﬁltration ([214, 215]), and the associated
graded space is a quotient of the space of functions on the arc space of the associated
scheme of the vertex algebra, as explained in Chapter 4 for further details. Thus, the
space of functions on an arc space will be the most crucial example of commutative
vertex algebras.
In later chapters, we will explore applications of the geometry of arc spaces to
vertex algebras. Conversely, vertex algebras also ﬁnd applications to arc spaces, see
[10, 33].
The chapter is organized as follows. Section 1.1 covers the jet construction of dif-
ferential algebras. Section 1.2, Section 1.3, and Section 1.4 address initial properties
and examples pertaining to arc schemes. We delve into the geometrical properties
17

=====PDF PAGE 20=====

of arc spaces in Section 1.5. In the context of vertex algebras, it’s also necessary to
consider the loop space L 푋of an aﬃne scheme 푋, discussed in Section 1.6. Arc
spaces of group schemes acting on a scheme are discussed in Section 1.7.

1.3 Arc spaces for general schemes
The following results are due to Greenberg ([151, 152]). We omit the proofs.
Theorem 1.2 (Greenberg) Let 푋be a scheme of ﬁnite type.
(i)
For any 푚∈Z⩾0 there exists a unique scheme J푚푋such that

for any commutative C-algebra 퐴. Equivalently,

for any scheme 푍.
(ii)
there exists a unique scheme J∞푋such that
HomSch(Spec 퐴, J∞푋)  HomSch(Spec 퐴[[푧]], 푋)
for any commutative C-algebra 퐴. Equivalently,
HomSch(푍, J∞푋)  HomSch(푍b×Spec C Spec C[[푧]], 푋)
for any scheme 푍, where 푍b×Spec C Spec C[[푧]] is the formal completion of
푍× Spec C[[푧]] with respect to 푍× {0}.

1.5 Geometric properties of arc spaces
It is known that the geometry of the jet schemes J푚(푋), for 푚⩾1, is closely linked
to that of 푋. More precisely, we can transport some geometrical properties from
J푚(푋) to 푋.
The following proposition gives examples of such phenomena.
Proposition 1.3 Let 푚∈Z⩾0, and let 푋be an aﬃne scheme of ﬁnite type. If J푚(푋) is
smooth (respectively, irreducible, reduced, normal, locally a complete intersection)
for some 푚, then so is 푋.
For smoothness, the converse is true, even with “every 푚” instead of “for some 푚”.
In fact, for smooth varieties, we have the following more precise statement, [104,
Corollary 2.11].
Proposition 1.4 If 푋is a smooth variety of dimension 푁, then the truncation mor-
phism 휋푚,푝, for 푝⩽푚, is a Zariski locally trivial projection with ﬁber isomorphic
to A(푚−푝) 푁. In particular, J푚(푋) is a smooth variety of dimension (푚+ 1)푁.
Proof Around every point in 푋we can ﬁnd an open subset 푈and an étale morphism
푈! A푁. Using Remark 1.1 the assertion reduced to the case where 푋is the aﬃne
space A푁, in which case the statement is clear by Section 1.1 and Section 1.2.
□

For the other properties stated in Proposition 1.3, the converse is not true in

It was suggested by Nash that the study of the geometry of J푚(푋), or of

In the same spirit, motivated by the case of the nilpotent cone of a reductive Lie
algebra (see Example 1.3), Eisenbud and Frenkel conjectured the following result,
proved by Mustaţă ([242]).
Theorem 1.3 Let 푋be an irreducible aﬃne variety over C.
(i)
If 푋is a complete intersection, then J푚(푋) is irreducible for every 푚⩾1 if
and only if 푋has rational singularities.
(ii)
If 푋is a complete intersection and if J푚(푋) is irreducible for some 푚⩾1,
then J푚(푋) is also reduced.
We have seen that jet schemes and arc spaces share several functorial properties.
For topological properties, they behave rather diﬀerently. The main reason is that
C[[푧]] is a domain, contrary to C[푧]/(푧푚+1). Thereby, although J∞(푋) is not of ﬁnite
type in general, its geometric properties are somehow simpler than those of the ﬁnite
jet schemes J푚(푋).

Consider now the general case. We argue by induction on 푑= dim 푋, the 푑= 0

1.7 Arc spaces of group schemes
A pro-algebraic group is an inverse limit of algebraic groups. We refer to [207]
for the basic theory of pro-algebraic groups (following [255]). About the theory of
group schemes we refer to [236] (referred to as algebraic group scheme or shortly
algebraic group in Milne’s book).

휋푚,푛: J푚퐺−! J푛퐺,

1 −! 퐾푚,푛−! J푚퐺

The advantage of the the view point adopted in Example 1.2 is that it gives
the explicit action of 픤[[푡]] = Lie(J∞퐺) on O(J∞퐺) as left-invariant vector ﬁelds,
see (1.17). The above view point is, nevertheless, useful to deﬁne the notion of
pro-J∞퐺-modules and the integrability condition with respect to J∞퐺.
Deﬁnition 1.1 Let 퐺be a linear algebraic group. A pro-module over J∞퐺is a group
module 휌: J∞퐺! GL(푉) for some complex vector space 푉such that any 푣∈푉
is contained in a ﬁnite-dimensional J∞퐺-submodule 푊of 푉satisfying for 푚large
enough:
• the group 퐾∞,푚acts trivially on 푊,
• the induced representation 휌푊
푚: J∞퐺/퐾∞,푚 J푚퐺! GL(푊) is algebraic.
Deﬁnition 1.2 Let 픤be any ﬁnite-dimensional Lie algebra. A pro-module over 픤[[푡]]
is a Lie algebra module 휋: 픤[[푡]] ! 픤픩(푉) for some complex vector space 푉such
that any 푣∈푉is contained in a ﬁnite-dimensional 픤[[푡]]-submodule 푊of 푉on
which 푡푚+1픤[[푡]] acts trivially on 푊for 푚large enough.
Note that a 픤[[푡]]-module 푉is a pro-module over 픤[[푡]] if and only if it is locally
ﬁnite and smooth, that it, for all 푣∈푉there is 푚⩾0 such that 푡푚+1픤[[푡]]푣= 0.
By diﬀerentiating, we obtain an obvious functor from the category of pro-modules

Deﬁnition 1.3 Let 퐺be a linear algebraic group, 픤= Lie(퐺). A pro-module (푉, 휋)

The following theorem is probably standard although we have not found any
reference: see [260] for further detail.
Theorem 1.6 Assume that 퐺is a connected reductive algebraic group, with 픤=
Lie(퐺). Let 푉be a smooth and locally ﬁnite module over 픤[[푡]]. Then 푉is J∞퐺-
integrable if and only if the following condition holds:
(1) the action of 푡픤[[푡]] is locally nilpotent,
(2) every ﬁnite-dimensional 픤-submodule 푊of푉is 퐺-integrable, that is, comes from
a structure of an algebraic 퐺-modules 퐺! GL(푊).
Remark 1.4 If 픤is semisimple and 퐺is simply-connected, then Condition (2) is
vacuous.

Chapter 2
Operator product expansion and vertex algebras
In this chapter, we collect the basic deﬁnitions (see Section 2.7) and standard prop-
erties of vertex algebras. We give several equivalent characterisation of the locality
axiom (cf. Section 2.3), which is the most important axiom of a vertex algebra,
and derive from this the Borcherds identities (see Section 2.3 and Section 2.8). The
easiest examples of vertex algebras are the commutatives vertex algebras which are
discussed in Section 2.10.
The ﬁrst interesting examples of noncommutative vertex algebras are given in
the next chapter (Chapter 3). Other important examples of noncommutative vertex
algebras will occur further in the book.
The best general references for this chapter are [122, 179, 95].

Theorem 2.2 (Wick’s formula) Let 푎1(푧), . . . , 푎푚(푧) and 푏1(푧), . . . , 푏푛(푧) be two
collections of ﬁelds such that the following properties hold:

[⟨푎푖, 푏푗⟩, 푎푘(푧)±] = 0 and [⟨푎푖, 푏푗⟩, 푏푘(푧)±] = 0 for all 푖, 푗, 푘;
(ii)
[푎푖(푧)±, 푏푗(푤)±] = 0 for all 푖and 푗.
Then one has the following OPE:
◦
◦푎1(푧) . . . 푎푚(푧) ◦

The theorem can be deduced from (2.29), and so from (2.24), by induction but we

Proof The typical term of the left-hand-side of (2.31) is
 푎푗1(푧)+푎푗2(푧)+ . . . 푎푖1(푧)−푎푖2(푧)−. . . 

Then we have to move the 푎푖(푧)−across the 푏푗(푤)+ in order to bring this product to
the normally ordered product as in (2.30). Due to the condition (ii) of the theorem,
we have

But due to condition (i), the contractions commute with all ﬁelds 푏푘(푤)±, hence can
be moved to the left. This proves the theorem.

2.7 Deﬁnition of vertex algebras
The following deﬁnition of a vertex algebra is due to De Sole and Kac [95].
Deﬁnition 2.5 A vector space 푉is a vertex algebra if it is equipped with a nonzero
vector |0⟩, an endomorphism 푇: 푉! 푉and a set of mutually local ﬁelds S on 푉
such that the following conditions holds:
(i)
푇|0⟩= 0 and [푇, 푎(푧)] = 휕푧푎(푧) for all 푎(푧) ∈S,
(ii)
푉is spanned by the vectors

(푛1) . . . 푎푖푚
(푛푚)|0⟩
for 푎푖1(푧), . . . , 푎푖푚(푧) ∈S written as 푎푖푗(푧) = Í
푛∈Z
푎푖푗
(푛)푧−푛−1.

Note that the second condition of (i) is equivalent to
[푇, 푎(푛)] = −푛푎(푛−1)

A vertex algebra is clearly a module over itself, which is called the adjoint
representation.
By deﬁnition, a subspace 푁of a 푉-module 푀is a submodule if 푎(푛)푁⊂푁for

A 푇-stable proper submodule of the adjoint representation is called an ideal of
푉. If 푓: 푉! 푉′ is a vertex algebra homomorphism, ker 푓is an ideal of 푉. For an
ideal 퐼of 푉, the quotient 푉/퐼inherits the vertex algebra structure from 푉. Indeed,
there are two ways to see this. One is to apply directly Deﬁnition 2.5 since 푉/퐼is

(Proposition 2.4), as it shows that 푌(푎, 푧)푏= 푒푧푇푌(푏, −푧)푎∈퐼for 푎∈퐼, 푏∈푉.
The category 푉/퐼-Mod is a full subcategory of 푉-Mod consisting of objects 푀
such that 푌푀(푎, 푧) = 0 for all 푎∈퐼.
2.10 Commutative vertex algebras
A vertex algebra 푉is called commutative if all vertex operators 푌(푎, 푧), 푎∈푉,
commute each other (i.e., we have 푁푎,푏= 0 in the locality axiom (2.39)). This
condition is equivalent to that
[푎(푚), 푏(푛)] = 0
for all
푎, 푏∈푉, 푚, 푛∈Z.
This condition is also equivalent to that 푎(푛)푏= 0 for all 푛⩾0, 푎, 푏∈푉, that
is, 푌(푎, 푧) ∈End푉[[푧]] for all 푎∈푉. Indeed, if 푌(푎, 푧) ∈End푉[[푧]] for all
푎∈푉then 푉is commutative by (2.40). Conversely, if 푉is commutative, then
푎(푛)푏= 푎(푛)푏(−1)|0⟩= 푏(−1)푎(푛)|0⟩= 0 for 푛⩾0.
Suppose that푉is commutative. Then, the relation (2.41) for 푚= 푛= −1 simpliﬁes
to (푎(−1)푏)(−1) = 푎(−1)푏(−1), that is,
(푎(−1)푏)(−1)푐= 푎(−1) (푏(−1)푐).
for all 푎, 푏, 푐∈푉. It follows that a commutative vertex algebra has a structure of a
unital commutative algebra with the product:
푎· 푏= 푎(−1)푏,
where the unit is given by the vacuum vector |0⟩. The translation operator 푇of 푉
acts on 푉as a derivation with respect to this product:
푇(푎· 푏) = (푇푎) · 푏+ 푎· (푇푏).
Therefore a commutative vertex algebra has the structure of a diﬀerential algebra,
see Section 1.1.
The converse holds according to the following exercice.

2.11 Vertex subalgebra, commutant and center
Deﬁnition 2.7 A subspace 푊of a vertex algebra 푉is called a vertex subalgebra if
|0⟩∈푊, 푇푊⊂푊, and 푎(푛)푏∈푊for all 푎, 푛∈푊, 푛∈Z.

Com(푊,푉) := {푣∈푉: [푤(푚), 푣(푛)] = 0 for all 푤∈푊, 푚, 푛∈Z},
= {푣∈푉: 푤(푛)푣= 0 for all 푤∈푊, 푛⩾0},
= {푣∈푉: 푣(푛)푤= 0 for all 푤∈푊, 푛⩾0}.
Let us explain the second equality. If 푤(푛)푣= 0 for all 푤∈푊, 푛⩾0, then 푣∈
Com(푊,푉) by (2.40). Conversely, if 푣∈Com(푊,푉) then 푤(푛)푣= 푤(푛)푣(−1)|0⟩=

It is straightforward to see that Com(푊,푉) is a vertex subalgebra of 푉, called the
commutant of 푊in 푉, or the coset of 푉by 푊.
Vertex subalgebras 푊1, 푊2 of 푉are said to form a dual pair if 푊1 = Com(푊2,푉)
and 푊2 = Com(푊1,푉). The commutant Com(푉,푉) of 푉in 푉is called the center of
푉, and it is denoted also by 푍(푉).

Chapter 3
Examples of noncommutative vertex algebras
We present in this chapter the ﬁrst important examples of noncommutative vertex
algebras: the universal aﬃne vertex algebras (cf. Section 3.1), that includes the
Heisenberg vertex algebras (see Example 3.1), the Virasoro vertex algebras (cf. Sec-
tion 3.2), and the chiral diﬀerential operators on an algebraic group (cf. Section 3.4)
These examples are all constructed from inﬁnite-dimensional Lie algebras. We will
see Part III more sophisticated examples, based on the quantized–Drinfeld reduction,
that generalize some of these examples.
It was observed that many vertex algebras that appear in the nature carry an action
of the Virasoro algebra, and satisfy a bounded-below property with respect to an
energy, or Hamiltonian, operator. This motivates the notion of conformal vertex alge-
bras and vertex operator algebras introduced in this chapter as well (cf. Section 3.3).
By considering quotients vertex algebras of these examples, that is, quotient by
vertex ideals, we construct many other interesting families of vertex algebras.

=====PDF PAGE 87=====
Part II
Poisson vertex algebras, Li ﬁltration and
associated varieties

=====PDF PAGE 88=====
This part is about important invariants attached to vertex algebras and the con-
nections between them.
Chapter 4 gives a concise presentation of Poisson vertex algebras (a particular
class of commutative vertex algebras). It will be noticed that any vertex algebra is
naturally ﬁltered and that the corresponding graded space has a structure of a Poisson
vertex algebra. The Zhu 퐶2-functor 푉7! 푅푉associates with any vertex algebra 푉
a certain quotient that has a Poisson algebra structure and generates the Poisson
vertex algebra gr푉as a diﬀerential algebra. The spectrum of the Zhu 퐶2-algebra
푅푉is called the associated scheme. Its maximal spectrum is called the associated
variety. Associated schemes and associated varieties, as well as their spaces of arcs,
are introduced in this chapter. They will occupy a central place in the rest of the
book. Not surprisingly, the associated variety is usually easier to describe than the
associated scheme as it will observed in many examples in the following parts. It
already contains meaningful information. One can, for example, detect from it the
lisse condition.
Zhu’s functor 푉7! Zhu(푉) is introduced in Chapter 5. It gives a correspondence
between the theory of modules over a vertex algebra and the representation theory
of its Zhu’s algebra. This correspondence is particularly well-understood in the case
of the universal aﬃne vertex algebras, where Zhu’s algebras are enveloping algebras
of the corresponding ﬁnite-dimensional simple Lie algebras.
In Chapter 6, we develop the theory of Poisson vertex modules and Frenkel–Zhu’s
bimodules. These notions generalize all above constructions to the setting of modules
over a vertex algebras.
Recall that Figure 0.1 in the introduction summarizes the main objects of this
part.
We do not claim that this diagram commutes. In general, only the upper right
triangle does. We will see, however, many interesting examples where all arrows
in Figure 0.1 commute, so that gr Zhu(푉)  푅푉. We will also discuss relations
between the Poisson vertex algebra gr푉and the coordinate ring over the arc space
of the associated scheme Spec 푅푉.

=====PDF PAGE 89=====
Chapter 4
Poisson vertex algebras
Recall that a Poisson algebra structure is a combination of a commutative associative
algebra structure and a Lie algebra structure with certain compatibility conditions.
We refer the reader to Appendix B for basics on Poisson algebras and Poisson
varieties. In the same spirit, a Poisson vertex algebra structure is a combination
of a commutative vertex algebra structure (or, equivalently, a diﬀerential algebra
structure) and a Lie vertex algebra structure, that is, a conformal Lie structure in
Kac’s terminology (this notion will be not discussed in the book). with natural
compatibility conditions.
In this book, we follow the approach of [50] using the 휆-bracket for the deﬁnition
of a Poisson vertex algebra. That point of view is, for example, particularly suited to
the study of integrable PDEs. We also present the 푛-th product approach.
Just as the graded space of an almost-commutative ﬁltered associative algebra with
unit have naturally a structure of a Poisson algebra, any vertex algebra is naturally
ﬁltered and the corresponding graded space is naturally a Poisson vertex algebra.
The corresponding scheme, referred to as the singular support, is of great interest.
In parallel, the 퐶2-coﬁniteness condition introduced by Zhu [282] is based on a
certain quotient, the Zhu 퐶2 quotient 푅푉, of a vertex algebras. This condition was
interpreted geometrically by the ﬁrst author in [14] using the variety 푋푉associated
with 푅푉. This variety turns out to be an extremely powerful invariant. To explore the
properties of the associated variety and the singular support is the main objective of
this part.
The chapter is structured as follows.
Section 4.1 is about the deﬁnition of Poisson vertex algebras. Section 4.2 is de-
voted to the Poisson vertex structure on arc spaces of Poisson schemes. The structure
of invariant arc spaces is discussed in Section 4.3. Section 4.4 is about the Li ﬁltra-
tion and the corresponding graded vertex algebras. Section 4.5 contains important
properties of the associated variety, the associated scheme and the singular support.
Various examples of such geometrical invariants are computed in Section 4.6. Sec-
tion 4.7 aims to compare the Li ﬁltration and the weight ﬁltration. The lisse condition
is discussed in Section 4.8. Finally, the chapter concludes with Section 4.9 on the
Poisson center of the Zhu 퐶2-algebra.
87

=====PDF PAGE 90=====

Corollary 4.1 Let {푎푖: 푖∈퐼} be a set of vectors of a good vertex algebra 푉. The
following are equivalent:
(i)

(ii)
the image of {푎푖: 푖∈퐼} generates 푅푉.
In particular, a vertex algebra 푉is ﬁnitely strongly generated if and only if 푅푉is
ﬁnitely generated.

generated.

Lemma 4.3 Let 푉, 푊be two Poisson vertex algebras, and 휙: 푉! 푊an algebra

휙(푎(푛)푏) = 휙(푎)(푛)휙(푏) for all 푛⩾0.

4.8 The lisse condition
Geometrical properties of the associated variety 푋푉should reﬂect important infor-
mation about the vertex algebra 푉. It is natural to ﬁrst consider the simplest case
where 푋푉has dimension 0.
Recall that we are assuming that a vertex algebra 푉is ﬁnitely strongly generated
so that ˜푋푉is a scheme of ﬁnite type. The following statement is mentioned in [48,
Exercise 8.3].

=====PDF PAGE 110=====
108
4 Poisson vertex algebras
Lemma 4.9 Let 푋= Spec 푅be an aﬃne scheme of ﬁnite type over a ﬁeld 퐾. Then
the following assertion are equivalent:
(i)
dim 푋= 0,
(ii)
푅is a ﬁnite dimensional 퐾-algebra.
If so, then 푋is a ﬁnite discrete topological space.
Proof To prove the equivalence (i)
⇐⇒
(ii), recall that a Noetherian ring has
dimension zero if and only if it is Artinian [228, Theorem 3.2 and Example 2 in §5].
So the converse implication (ii) ⇒(i) is clear because a ﬁnite dimensional algebra
is an Artinian ring. Indeed, if 푅is a ﬁnite dimensional 퐾-vector space, then it is
Artinian as 퐾-vector space. But every ideal of 푅is a 퐾-vector space and thus they
satisfy the descending chain condition, which proves that 푅is Artinian as ring.
Conversely, assume that dim 푋= 0, that is, 푅is Artinian. Then 푅is a ﬁnite
product of Artinian local rings (cf. [48, Theorems 8.7]). So one may assume that 푅
is an Artinian local ring, with maximal ideal 픪. Then 푅/픪if a ﬁnite extension of 퐾
by Zariski lemma. Since 푅is Artinian, 픪is the radical of 퐴([228, proof of Theorem
3.2]) and thus 픪푛= 0 for some 푛. Thus we have a chain
푅⊇픪⊇픪2 ⊇· · · ⊇픪푛= 0.
Since 푅is Noetherian, 픪is ﬁnitely generated and each 픪푖/픪푖+1 is a ﬁnite di-
mensional 푅/픪-vector space, hence 픪is a ﬁnite dimensional vector space. This
completes the proof.
□
Deﬁnition 4.11 A vertex algebra 푉is called lisse (or 퐶2-coﬁnite) if dim 푋푉= 0 or,
equivalently, if 푅푉= 푉/퐹1(푉) is ﬁnite dimensional.
As a consequence of Proposition 4.5, we have the following result.
Theorem 4.4 We have dim 푆푆(푉) = 0 if and only if dim 푋푉= 0.
Proof The “only if” part is obvious since 휋∞(푆푆(푉)) = ˜푋푉. The “if” part follows
from Corollary 1.2 and Proposition 4.5.
□
By Theorem 4.4 we get:
Lemma 4.10 The vertex algebra 푉is lisse if and only if dim 푆푆(푉) = 0.
The 퐶2-coﬁniteness condition, dim푉/퐶2(푉) < ∞, was introduced by Zhu [282];
the same condition was studied earlier by Feigin and Fuchs [115] in the context
of the Virasoro vertex algebra. The term lisse has been borrowed from Beilinson,
Feigin, and Mazur, who considered the ﬁniteness condition dim 푆푆(푉) = 0 in the
context of Virasoro vertex algebras. The equivalence between these two notions was
established in [14]. In this book, we will be using the term lisse instead.
Lemma 4.11 Suppose that 푉is conical, so that 푉= É
Δ⩾0 푉Δ and 푉0 = C|0⟩. The
algebras gr퐹푉and 푅푉are equipped with the induced grading:

Chapter 5
Modules over vertex algebras and Zhu’s functor
We introduce in this chapter the Zhu functor 푉7! Zhu(푉) assigning to a vertex
operator algebra the associative algebra Zhu(푉). Zhu established that the equivalence
classes of the irreducible representations of 푉are in one-to-one correspondence with
the equivalence classes of the irreducible representations of Zhu(푉). The associative
algebra Zhu(푉) has a much simpler structure than 푉, for example, the one-to-one
correspondence theorem implies that if 푉is rational then Zhu(푉) is semisimple.
The algebra Zhu(푉) also plays a crucial role in the proof of the modular invariance
of characters [282, 237].
Section 5.1 is about Zhu’s functor. An alternative description of Zhu’s algebra is
given in Section 5.2. The ﬁrst important theorem of the chapter (Theorem 5.2) is
proven in Section 5.3 using this alternative description. In Section 5.4, we discuss
the connexion between the Zhu algebra and the Zhu 퐶2-algebra. The second crucial
result of the chapter (Theorem 5.6) is proved in Section 5.5. To ﬁnish, using the
technics of Section 5.4, we explicitly compute in Section 5.6 the Zhu algebra in
some examples.
We continue to assume that a vertex algebra 푉is ﬁnitely strongly generated.
5.1 Zhu’s algebra and Zhu’s functor
We assume in this chapter that 푉be a Z-graded vertex algebra (see Deﬁnition 2.8).

Remark 5.1 There is also an alternative construction of Zhu’s algebra by van Ekeren
and Heluani that uses elliptic functions and does not use the conformal structure
[102]. As a matter of fact, Zhu’s algebra structure is independent of the conformal
structure.
The importance of Zhu’s algebra in vertex algebra theory comes from the follow-
ing fact [282].
Theorem 5.2 (Zhu) Assume that 푉is Z-graded. For any positive energy represen-
tation 푀of 푉, [푎] 7! 표(푎) gives a well deﬁned representation of Zhu(푉) on 푀top,
where [푎] is the image of 푎in Zhu(푉). Moreover, the correspondence 푀7! 푀top
gives a bĳection between the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible positive energy
representations of 푉and that of simple Zhu(푉)-modules.
The proof of this theorem will be given after Theorem 5.3.
Remark 5.2 When the vertex algebra 푉is a 1
2Z-graded, one usually considers two
kinds of representations, both equally important: Neveu-Schwarz representations
and Ramond twisted representations. Neveu-Schwarz representations are usual non-
twisted representations of 푉, which are 1
2Z-graded, while Ramond twisted represen-
tations are certain twisted representations of 푉, which are Z-graded. More precisely,
Ramond twisted representations are twisted Z⩾0-graded 푉-modules for the automor-
phism of 푉that acts as the identity on the Z⩾0-graded vertex operator subalgebra
and as −1 on the (Z⩾0 + 1
2)-graded subspace [95], see also [13]. Accordingly, there
are two kinds of Zhu’s algebras. One is to parametrize the simple positive energy
Neveu-Schwarz representations of푉, whose deﬁnition is given in [183]. Another one
is to to parametrize the simple positive energy Ramond twisted representations of
푉, whose deﬁnition is given by the same formula as (5.1). We call the corresponding
Zhu algebra, the Ramond twisted Zhu algebra of 푉, and we still denote it by Zhu(푉).

The map 휂푉is an isomorphism in several examples: the universal aﬃne ver-

the universal W-algebra W 푘(픤, 푓) (cf. Section 9.2.1), etc. However, it is not an
isomorphism in general.

5.6 Computation of Zhu’s algebras
This section describes some technics to compute the Zhu algebra, and contains some
explicit examples.
5.6.1 PBW basis
Recall that a vertex algebra 푉admits a PBW basis if 푅푉is a polynomial algebra and
if the map C[J∞( ˜푋푉)] ↠gr퐹푉is an isomorphism (cf. Deﬁnition 4.9).
Theorem 5.8 If 푉admits a 푃퐵푊basis, then 휂푉: 푅푉↠gr(Zhu푉) is an isomor-
phism.
Proof We have gr Zhu(푉) = 푉/gr(푉◦푉), where gr(푉◦푉) is the associated graded
space of 푉◦푉with respect to the ﬁltration induced by the ﬁltration 푉⩽푝. We wish to
show that gr(푉◦푉) = 퐹1푉. Since 푎◦푏≡푎(−2)푏(mod 푉⩽Δ푎+Δ푏) for homogeneous
푎, 푏∈푉, it is suﬃcient to show that 푎◦푏≠0 implies that 푎(−2)푏≠0.
Suppose that 푎(−2)푏= (푇푎)(−1)푏= 0 for homogeneous 푎, 푏∈푉. Since 푉admits
a PBW basis, gr퐹푉has no zero divisors, whence 푇푎= 0. Also, from the PBW
property we ﬁnd that 푇푎= 0 implies that 푎= 푐|0⟩for some constant 푐∈C. Thus, 푎
is a constant multiple of |0⟩, in which case 푎◦푏= 0.
□
5.6.2 Universal aﬃne vertex algebras
The universal aﬃne vertex algebra 푉푘(픤) admits a PBW basis. Therefore
휂푉푘(픤) : 푅푉푘(픤) = C[픤∗]

gives a well deﬁned algebra homomorphism. This map respects the ﬁltration on both

=====PDF PAGE 143=====
Part III
BRST cohomology and quantum
Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction

=====PDF PAGE 144=====
Kostant and Sternberg [205], inspired by Feigin [114], suggested a BRST coho-
mological interpretation of the Hamiltonian reduction.
This part studies the BRST cohomology in various contexts. Chapter 7 is within
the framework of Poisson algebras. Applying the BRST reduction in a special case,
we obtain a BRST interpretation of the Slodowy slices and equivariant Slodowy
slices that also appear in the study of singularities of nilpotent orbit closures (see
Appendix D.6 in appendix about this topic).
In Chapter 8 we consider the quantized BRST cohomology. In this setting, we
construct the ﬁnite W-algebras using BRST complexes. This follows the works of
Kostant [204], further generalized by Lynch [225] and developed by Premet [248]
and Gan–Ginzburg [138]. Chapter 8 also covers some aspects of the representation
theory of ﬁnite W-algebras, following the works of Premet and Losev.
Chapter 9 is the heart of the part and is about the chiral quantized BRST co-
homology. This allows to deﬁnes the aﬃne W-algebras, simply often referred to
as W-algebras, and the equivariant W-algebras. Chapter 10 presents an alternative
description of the BRST reduction which allows to give the deﬁnition of W-algebras
following the Kac–Roan–Wakimoto approach [182].

=====PDF PAGE 145=====
Chapter 7
BRST cohomology and Slodowy slices
In this chapter we shall introduce the BRST cohomology (where BRST refers to the
physicists Becchi, Rouet, Stora and Tyutin) for later purpose.
In more details, Section 7.1 is about the general BRST cohomology. Applying this
construction to the BRST cohomology associated with nilpotent elements of a simple
Lie algebra, we obtain in Section 7.2 the BRST realization of Slodowy slices (the
Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction). Section 7.3 is about equivariant Slodowy slices. While
Slodowy slices are merely (non symplectic) Poisson varieties, equivariant Slodowy
slices are always symplectic varieties. In fact, we will see that the former are obtained
from O(픤∗), for simple 픤= Lie(퐺), by Hamiltonian reduction while the later are
obtained from O(푇∗퐺)  O(퐺) ⊗O(픤∗), where 푇∗퐺is the cotangent bundle 퐺
which is clearly symplectic. The Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction allows us to deﬁne in
Section 7.4 the Moore–Tachikawa operation on the set of Poisson varieties acted by
a simple Lie group with Hamiltonian actions. Lastly, we extend in Section 7.5 the
BRST reduction to the setting of Poisson modules over O(픤∗) (here, 픤is an arbitrary
algebraic Lie algebra). Then we apply this to the special case of the Drinfeld–Sokolov
reduction.
We refer the reader to Appendix B about basics on Poissons algebras and to Ap-
pendix E for backgrounds on superspaces, superalgebras, Lie superalgebras, etc. and
Cliﬀord algebras. About spectral sequences, we refer to Appendix F (for more details,
see [281, Chapter 5]).
7.1 BRST cohomology and BRST reduction
7.1.1 BRST cohomology
Let 퐺be any connected aﬃne algebraic group, 픤= Lie(퐺) (we do not assume that 퐺
is semisimple). Let {푥푖}1⩽푖⩽푑a basis of 픤, and let {푥∗

linear map 픤× 픤푒! 픤, (푣, 푤) 7! 푔([푣, 푥]) + 푔(푤). So 푑휂(Id, 푓) (푣, 푤) = [푣, 푓] + 푤

=====PDF PAGE 167=====
Chapter 8
Quantized BRST cohomology and ﬁnite
W-algebras
The goal of this chapter is to quantize the BRST constructions made in the previous
chapter. Then, we will proceed with the study of ﬁnite W-algebras. These algebras
were introduced by Premet and have attracted attention for several reasons, mostly
related to classical problems of representation theory. For our purpose, they are
important as (Ramond twisted) Zhu’s algebras of (aﬃne) W-algebras, as we will see
in the next chapter. Finite W-algebras are certain generalizations of the enveloping
algebra of a simple Lie algebra. They can be deﬁned through the BRST cohomol-
ogy associated with nilpotent elements. So, the deﬁnition and properties of ﬁnite
W-algebras are deeply related to the geometry of nilpotent orbits. We recall that Ap-
pendix D gathers standard facts on nilpotent elements and nilpotent orbits in simple
Lie algebras.
The chapter is structured as follows. Section 8.1, Section 8.2, Section 8.3 and
Section 8.4 concern the quantizations of the BRST constructions made in Section 7.1,
Section 7.2, Section 7.3 and Section 7.4, respectively, essentially following [205].
In particular, we start in Section 8.1 with a general setting. After that, we focus
from Section 8.2 on the special BRST reduction associated with nilpotent elements
of a simple Lie algebra. In Section 8.5, we collect various important results on the
representation theory of ﬁnite W-algebras.
8.1 Quantized BRST cohomology and quantized BRST reduction
Let 퐺be any connected aﬃne algebraic group with Lie algebra 픤, and let 휒: 픤! C
be a character, that is, a one-point 퐺-orbit.

=====PDF PAGE 168=====
166

By Lemma 8.3, the component group 퐶( 푓) acts on the set of all one-dimensional
representations of 푈(픤, 푓). By showing that the ﬁxed point set is nonempty, Premet
proved the following result ([251]).
Theorem 8.11 (Premet) Any ﬁnite W-algebra admits a one-dimensional represen-
tation (equivalently, a two-sided ideal of codimension one).

Remark 9.6 For a general simple Lie algebra 픤, the image of W푘(픤) by the Miura

injectivity of the Miura map.

with an arbitrary nilpotent element 푓.

=====PDF PAGE 215=====
Part IV
Quasi-lisse vertex algebras and W-algebras

=====PDF PAGE 216=====
As a Poisson variety, the associated variety of a vertex algebra is a ﬁnite disjoint
union of smooth analytic Poisson manifolds, and it is stratiﬁed by its symplectic
leaves. The vertex algebras whose associated variety has ﬁnitely many symplectic
leaves are particularly matter of interest and were ﬁrst considered by the authors in
[42]. They were then referred to as quasi-lisse vertex algebras in [32].
Deﬁnition 10.1 A ﬁnitely strongly generated Z⩾0-graded vertex algebra 푉is called
quasi-lisse if 푋푉has only ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves.
This part is devoted to the study of quasi-lisse vertex algebras. Lisse vertex
algebras appear as special cases quasi-lisse vertex algebras: they are the vertex al-
gebras whose associated variety is just a point (remember that all vertex algebras
are assumed to be strongly generated and Z⩾0-graded). By a result of Etingof–
Schedler [109], the quasi-lisse condition implies that the zeroth Poisson homology
푅푉/{푅푉, 푅푉} is ﬁnite-dimensional. This weaker condition is crucial in many ap-
plications.
We give various examples of quasi-lisse vertex algebras, and present remarkable
properties of lisse and quasi-lisse vertex algebras. Our ﬁrst examples are particular
cases of simple aﬃne vertex algebras. Using the results of Part III one can construct
plenty of other examples in the context of W-algebras by taking the quantized
Drinfeld-Sokolov reduction of quasi-lisse aﬃne vertex algebras. There are also other
expected examples coming from four dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld
theories.
As a ﬁrst motivation, let us comment the quasi-lisse condition in the setting of
aﬃne vertex algebras.
We have seen that 푉푘(픤) plays a role similar to that of the enveloping algebra
of 픤for the representation theory of the aﬃne Kac-Moody algebra ˆ픤. It would be
nice to have analogues of the associated varieties of primitive ideals in this context
(see Appendix D.5). Unfortunately, one cannot expect exactly the same theory. One
of the main reasons is that the center of 푈(ˆ픤) is trivial (unless for the critical level
푘= −ℎ∨), and so we do not have analogue of the nilpotent cone (for the critical level,
the analogue is played by the arc space of the nilpotent cone, see Example 1.3 and
Exercice 3.2). So we need some replacements. In this context, the associated variety
of the highest weight irreducible representation 퐿(푘Λ0) = 퐿푘(픤) of ˆ픤, 푘∈C, viewed
as a vertex algebra, is a right object. More generally, one can consider the associated
variety of any irreducible highest representation 퐿(휆) of ˆ픤, by exploiting the notion
of associated variety for any module over a vertex algebra; see Section 6.3. We will
see next chapters some analogies between the associated variety of 퐿푘(픤) and the
associated variety of primitive ideals. However, there are substantial diﬀerences. For
example, since 퐿푘(픤)  푉푘(픤) for 푘∉Q (cf. [181]), we see that 푋퐿푘(픤) is not always
contained in the nilpotent cone N of 픤. We observe that 푋퐿푘(픤) is contained in the
nilpotent cone N if and only if 퐿푘(픤) is quasi-lisse (see Proposition 12.1). Thus, in
this context, the quasi-lisse condition looks very natural.
The part is structured as follows. It starts with Chapter 11 on lisse and rational
vertex algebras. Chapter 12 contains various examples of quasi-lisse (simple) vertex
algebras, mostly n the context of aﬃne vertex algebras and W-algebras Remarkable

=====PDF PAGE 217=====
215
properties of quasi-lisses vertex algebras are described in Chapter 13. In fact, the
vertex algebras constructed from 4D N = 2 SCFTs are expected to be quasi-lisse,
since their associated varieties conjecturally coincide with the Higgs branches of the
corresponding four dimensional theories ([253]).

=====PDF PAGE 219=====
Chapter 11
Lisse and rational vertex algebras
Before discussing the case of quasi-lisse vertex algebras, we focus in this chapter
on lisse and rational vertex algebras. Recall that a vertex algebra 푉is called lisse
if dim 푋푉= 0, or equivalently, if 푅푉is ﬁnite-dimensional or, still equivalently, if
dim 푆푆(푉) = 0 (see Section 4.8). Close to the lisse condition, we have the rationality
condition:
Deﬁnition 11.1 A conformal vertex algebra 푉is called rational if every Z⩾0-graded
푉-modules is completely reducible (that is, isomorphic to a direct sum of simple
푉-modules).
Examples of lisse vertex algebras are given in Section 11.1 and Section 11.2.
These examples of lisse vertex algebras are actually also examples of rational vertex
algebras. In Section 11.3, we gather together remarkable properties that enjoy lisse
and rational vertex algebras.
11.1 Integrable representations of aﬃne Kac-Moody algebras
Let 픤be a complex simple Lie algebra. The irreducible 픤-representation 퐿픤(휆), with
highest weight 휆∈픥∗, is ﬁnite-dimensional if and only if its associated variety
V (Ann푈(픤) (퐿픤(휆))) is zero (Example D.6). Contrary to irreducible highest weight
representations of 픤, the irreducible ˆ픤-representation 퐿(휆), where 휆∈ˆ픥∗, is ﬁnite-
dimensional if and only if 휆= 0, that is, 퐿(휆) is the trivial representation.
The notion of ﬁnite-dimensional representations has to be replaced by the no-
tion of integrable representations in the category O. (See Appendix A.4 and Ap-
pendix A.5 for the category O and the deﬁnition of integrable representations for
aﬃne Kac-Moody algebras.)
Let 푘∈C. Recall that the simple aﬃne vertex algebra 퐿푘(픤) is isomorphic to the
irreducible highest weight representation 퐿(푘Λ0) as a ˆ픤-module.

11.3 On the lisse and the rational conditions
It is known ([97]) that the rationality condition implies that푉has ﬁnitely many simple

(cf. Theorem 5.6). Moreover, the normalized characters of the ﬁnitely simple Z⩾0-

properties ([282, 237]). All this is summarized in the below theorem.

(iii) Assume furthermore that 푉is rational. Then the linear space spanned by the

The converse is not true. It is known that a lisse vertex algebra is not necessarily
rational. There are examples in the context of Z2-orbibolds ([1]). It is conjectured is

with a minimal nilpotent element 푓min is lisse and non-rational for a nonnegative
integer 푘.
There are signiﬁcant vertex algebras that do not satisfy the lisse condition. For
instance, an admissible aﬃne vertex algebra 퐿푘(픤) (see Section 12.2) has a complete
reducibility property ([18]), and the modular invariance property ([185]) in the
category O still holds, although it is not lisse unless it is integrable.
So it is natural to try to relax the lisse condition. This is the purpose of the next
chapters.

Chapter 12
Examples of quasi-lisse vertex algebras
This chapter contains various examples of quasi-lisse vertex algebras. Section 12.1
is about general facts on the associated variety of aﬃne vertex algebras. Then we
focus on two interesting families of quasi-lisse simple aﬃne vertex algebras: those
coming from admissible levels (Section 12.2) and those coming from the Deligne
exceptional series (Section 12.3). We refer to Remark 12.2 for other known family of
examples; they are certainly much more examples. By taking the Drinfeld–Sokolov
reduction of all previous examples, we produce in Section 12.4 many other examples
of quasi-lisse vertex algebras in the context of W-algebras. A few of other examples
are discussed in Section 12.5.
In what follows, 픤is a complex simple Lie algebra with adjoint group 퐺, and N
is the nilpotent cone of 픤, that is, the set of nilpotent elements of 픤. We identify 픤
with its dual 픤∗using the non-degenerate bilinear form (−|−) = 1
ℎ∨× Killing form
of 픤. We shall use the notations of Appendix D, particularly for the nilpotent orbits
in 픰픩푛in correspondence with the partitions of 푛.
12.1 General facts on associated varieties of aﬃne vertex algebras
Let 푉푘(픤) be the universal aﬃne vertex algebra associated with 픤at the level 푘∈C.
Recall ﬁrst that the associated variety of 푉푘(픤) is 픤∗ 픤(cf. Example 4.5).
Let us now focus on the associated variety of the simple quotient 퐿푘(픤) =
푉푘(픤)/푁푘, where 푁푘is the maximal proper submodule of 푉푘(픤). Contrary to the
associated varieties of primitive ideals of 푈(픤), the associated variety of 퐿푘(픤) is not
always contained in the nilpotent cone N. Indeed, if 푉푘(픤) is simple, for example if
푘∉Q, then 퐿푘(픤) = 푉푘(픤) and so 푋퐿푘(픤) = 픤⊄N. The main result of [34] ensures
that the converse is true as well.
Theorem 12.1 (Arakawa–Jiang–Moreau) Let 푘∈C. Then 푋퐿푘(픤) = 픤if and only
if 퐿푘(픤) = 푉푘(픤), that is, 푉푘(픤) is simple.
221

=====PDF PAGE 224=====
222
12 Examples of quasi-lisse vertex algebras
On the other hand, we have a simple criterion to check whether 퐿푘(픤) is quasi-
lisse:
Proposition 12.1 The simple aﬃne vertex algebra 퐿푘(픤) is quasi-lisse if and only if
푋퐿푘(픤) ⊂N. In particular, 푉푘(픤) is never quasi-lisse.
Proof Recall that the symplectic leaves of 픤are the adjoint 퐺-orbits of 픤. Since the
works of Kostant, we know that the nilpotent cone N of the simple Lie algebra is a
ﬁnite union of adjoint orbits (see Appendix D.1). Hence, if 푋퐿푘(픤) is contained in N
then 퐿푘(픤) is quasi-lisse.
Conversely, assume that 푋퐿푘(픤) contains a non-nilpotent element 푥, with Jordan
decomposition 푥= 푥푠+ 푥푛. If 푥푛= 0, then 푋퐿푘(픤) contains 퐺C∗푥= 퐺C∗푥푠, using
the fact that 푋퐿푘(픤) is a closed 퐺-invariant cone of 픤. But 퐺C∗푥푠contains inﬁnitely
many symplectic leaves because 푥푠is semisimple. So 푋퐿푘(픤) is not quasi-lisse. If
푥푛≠0, choose an 픰픩2-triple (푥푛, ℎ, 푦푛) in 픤푥푠; one can assume that ℎand 푥푠lie in a
same Cartan subalgebra (because 푥푠lies in the center of 픤푥푠) so that they commute.
Consider the one-parameter subgroup 휌: C∗! 퐺generated by ad ℎ. We have for
all 푡∈C∗,
휌(푡)푥= 푥푠+ 푡2푥푛.
Taking the limit when 푡goes to 0, we deduce that 푥푠∈푋퐿푘(픤) and, hence, by the ﬁrst
case, 푋퐿푘(픤) is not quasi-lisse.
□
The proof of the following proposition uses the inﬁnitesimal character of a highest
representation.
Proposition 12.2 If 푋퐿푘(픤) ⊂N, then 퐿푘(픤) has only ﬁnitely many simple objects
in the category O.

Using the surjective Poisson algebra homomorphism (cf. Lemma 5.11),
푅퐿푘(픤) −↠gr Zhu(퐿푘(픤)) = gr푈(픤)/gr 퐼,

Deﬁnition 12.1 The sheets of 픤∗are by deﬁnition the irreducible components of the

dimension 7 while 픤has dimension 8). More generally, for any regular semisimple

[86, Th. 2.9]). The principal cone X is somehow more canonical: it is precisely the

12.3 Exceptional Deligne series
There was actually a “strong Feigin–Frenkel conjecture” stating that 푘is admissible
if and only if 푋퐿푘(픤) ⊂N. Such a statement would be interesting because it would
give a geometrical description of the admissible representations 퐿푘(픤). As seen in
Exercise 12.3, the equivalence holds for 픤= 픰픩2.
12.3.1 Non-admissible quasi-lisse aﬃne vertex algebras

12.3.2 Joseph ideal and proof of Theorem 12.5
We refer the reader to Appendix D.5 for standard facts on primitive ideals and their
associated varieties.
If 픤is not of type 퐴, it is known [172, 139] that there exists a unique completely
prime ideal, that is, the corresponding graded ideal is prime, in 푈(픤) whose associ-
ated variety is the minimal nilpotent orbit Omin, which is the unique nilpotent orbit

Deﬁnition 12.2 If 픤is not of type 퐴, the unique completely prime ideal whose
associated variety is Omin is denoted by J0, and is referred to as the Joseph ideal of
푈(픤).
For 픤of type 퐴, the completely prime primitive ideals 퐼of푈(픤) with V (퐼) = Omin
form a single family parametrized by the elements of C ([172, 250]). In [172],
Joseph has also computed the inﬁnitesimal character of J0, that is, the algebra
homomorphism 푍(픤) ! C through which the centre 푍(픤) acts on the primitive
quotient 푈(픤)/J0. In fact, Joseph has described the set of 휆∈픥∗such that such that
J0 = Ann푈(픤) (퐿픤(휆)) (see Table 12.1).
Outside the type 퐴the nilpotent orbit Omin is rigid1, hence forms a single sheet
(cf. Deﬁnition 12.1) in 픤∗ 픤. Indeed, a nilpotent orbit forms a single sheet if and
only if it is rigid. So, J0 cannot be obtained by parabolic induction from a primitive
ideal of a proper Levi subalgebra of 픤. Diﬀerent realizations of J0 can be found in
the literature for various types of 픤. Joseph’s original proof of the uniqueness of J0
was incomplete. This led Gan and Savin [139] to give another description of the
Joseph ideal J0. Their argument relies on some invariant theory and earlier results
of Garﬁnkle.

Kostant, J0 is generated by the 픤-submodule 퐿픤(0) ⊕푊in 푆2(픤), where W is such

Chapter 13
Properties of quasi-lisse vertex algebras
In this chapter, it is assumed that 푉is a strongly generated Z⩾0-graded vertex algebra
such that 푉0  C|0⟩. Such vertex algebras are sometimes said of CFT-type. Recall
that 푋푉is called quasi-lisse is 푋푉has only ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves. We have
already noticed that lisse vertex algebras are very special (see e.g., Lemma 4.10 and
Theorem 11.3). This chapter explores interesting properties of the larger class of
quasi-lisse vertex algebras.
It is known that Poisson varieties with only ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves
enjoy remarkable properties. For example, Brown and Gordon have proved [76]
that a ﬁnite number of symplectic leaves in a Poisson variety 푋implies that the
symplectic leaf L푥at 푥∈푋coincides with the regular locus of the zero variety of
the maximal Poisson ideal contained in the maximal ideal 픪푥corresponding to 푥(see
Section 13.3 for more details about this). Thus, each symplectic leaf L푥is a smooth
connected locally-closed algebraic subvariety in 푋. In particular, every irreducible
component of 푋is the closure of a symplectic leaf [145, Corollary 3.3]. On the other
hand, it has been established by Etingof and Schedler [109] that if 푅is a ﬁnitely
generated Poisson algebra such that 푋= Specm(푅) has ﬁnitely many symplectic
leaves, then the 0-th Poisson cohomology 푅/{푅, 푅} is ﬁnite-dimensional. As one
can expect, these important facts play an important role in the study of quasi-lisse
vertex algebras. This is what we shall see in this chapter.
Section 13.1 is about the modular invariance properties of quasi-lisse vertex
algebras. In Section 13.3, we introduce the notion of chiral symplectic core and
exploit them to show that any quasi-lisse vertex algebra 푉is a quantization of the
reduced arc space of its associated variety, in the sense that its reduced singular
support Spec(gr퐹푉) coincides with J∞(푋푉) as topological spaces (Theorem 13.5).
Finally, Section 13.2 concerns the irreducibility conjecture for the associated variety
of quasi-lisse vertex algebras (Conjecture 13.3) and its connection with the Higgs
branch conjecture in four-dimensional N = 2 super-conformal theories.
235

=====PDF PAGE 238=====
236
13 Properties of quasi-lisse vertex algebras
13.1 Modular invariance property of quasi-lisse vertex algebras
Let 푉be a conformal vertex algebra and 푀a 푉-module 푀. As usual, we set
푀푑:= {푚∈푀: 퐿0푚= 푑푚}. The 퐿0-eigenvalue of a nonzero 퐿0-eigenvector
푚∈푀is called its conformal weight. For an element 푎∈푉of conformal weight Δ
we write 푎(푧) = Í

13.2 Irreducibility conjecture
Taking all examples of quasi-lisse vertex algebras as in Chapter 12 into consideration
we formulate in [41] a conjecture.
Conjecture 13.3 Let 푉= ⊕푑⩾0푉푑be a simple, ﬁnitely strongly generated, positively
graded conformal vertex operator algebra such that 푉0  C|0⟩. Assume that 푋푉has
ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves, that is, 푉is quasi-lisse. Then 푋푉is irreducible.
In particular, if the associated variety 푋퐿푘(픤) of the simple aﬃne vertex algebra
퐿푘(픤) at level 푘is contained in the nilpotent cone N , the conjecture stipulates that
푋퐿푘(픤) must be the closure of some nilpotent orbit in 픤.
Thus, Conjecture 13.3 in particular says that any quasi-lisse simple aﬃne vertex
algebra produces exactly one lisse simple W-algebra. Indeed, assume that 푋퐿푘(픤) = O
for some nilpotent orbit O = 퐺. 푓in 픤. Then the W-algebra 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) is lisse by
Theorem 12.9 (ii), and so is the simple W-algebra W푘(픤, 푓).

=====PDF PAGE 243=====
13.2 Irreducibility conjecture
241
13.2.1 On the irreducibility of nilpotent Slodowy slices
Conjecture 13.3 has been veriﬁed for the known cases of quasi-lisse vertex algebras
in [42] as described in Chapter 12. This is a subtle problem for the associated
varieties of the Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction of examples of known simple quasi-lisse
aﬃne vertex algebras (see Section 12.4) for which deep results on the geometry of
nilpotent orbits are needed for the veriﬁcations. We refer the reader to Appendix D.6
in appendix for more details about the geometry of nilpotent Slodowy slices, in
particular the unibranchness.
To be more speciﬁc, by Theorem 12.9 (iii), the W-algebra 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) is quasi-
lisse if 푋퐿푘(픤) is contained in the nilpotent cone N. Assuming Conjecture 13.3 true,
the associated variety of 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) is in fact the nilpotent Slodowy slice O ∩S 푓
for some nilpotent orbit O. This variety is known to be irreducible for all 푓∈O if
and only if O is unibranch at all 푓∈O.
It is veriﬁed in [42] that all nilpotent orbit closures appearing as associated
varieties of known quasi-lisse simple aﬃne vertex algebras, that is, the simple aﬃne
vertex algebras at admissible levels (for which the associated varieties are described
in Tables 2–10 of [17, Table 4]) plus those listed in Section 12.3.1, are unibranch.
They are all normal (and so unibranch) in the simple Lie algebras of classical types,
and all unibranch in the simple Lie algebras of exceptional types. Note that the
nilpotent orbit ˜퐴1 of 퐺2 of dimension 8 is not normal [200], but unibranch, and
appears as associated variety of 퐿푘(퐺2) for some 푘with denominator 2 ([17, Table
4]).
Many other simple aﬃne vertex algebras (at non-admissible levels) are expected
to be quasi-lisse. So the above discussion is a sort of veriﬁcation that Conjecture 13.3
is highly plausible.
13.2.2 Ginzburg’s irreducibility theorem
In another direction, Conjecture 13.3 is a natural aﬃne analog of the irreducibility
theorem (cf. Theorem D.5) for the associated variety of primitive ideals of 푈(픤),
which has been generalized to a larger class of Noetherian algebras in [144]:
Theorem 13.4 (Ginzburg) Let 퐴be a ﬁltered unital C-algebra. Assume furthermore
that gr 퐴 C[푋] is the coordinate ring of a reduced irreducible aﬃne algebraic
variety 푋, and assume that the Poisson variety Spec(gr 퐴) has only ﬁnitely many
symplectic leaves. Then for any primitive ideal 퐼⊂퐴, the zero locus V(퐼) of gr 퐼in
푋is the closure of a single symplectic leaf. In particular, it is irreducible.
Ginzburg’s proof of Theorem 13.4 is an adaptation of a more direct proof of
Theorem D.5 discovered subsequently by Vogan [273], combined with the results
by Brown and Gordon [76] on symplectic cores. Motivated by Ginzburg’s theorem
and Conjecture 13.3, we introduced in [43] the notion of chiral symplectic cores (see
Deﬁnition 13.3). This is the topic of the next section.

=====PDF PAGE 244=====

13.3 Chiral symplectic cores and applications
Let 푋= Spec 푅be a reduced Poisson scheme. For 퐼an ideal of 푅, we denote by
P푅(퐼) the biggest Poisson ideal of 푅contained in 퐼. The symplectic core C푅(푥) of
a point 푥∈푋is the equivalence class of 푥for ∼, with
푥∼푦⇐⇒P푅(픪푥) = P푅(픪푦).
Here, 픪푥stands for the maximal ideal of 푅corresponding to 푥. The notion of
symplectic cores, introduced in [76], are expected to be the ﬁnest possible algebraic
stratiﬁcation in which the Hamiltonian vector ﬁelds are tangent. Brown and Gordon
showed that the symplectic cores coincide with the symplectic leaves, if there is only
ﬁnitely many numbers of symplectic leaves.
13.3.1 Chiral symplectic cores
It is natural to try to adapt this notion to the context of Poisson vertex algebras.
Assume for awhile that 푉is a Poisson vertex algebra. Let 퐼be an ideal of 푉in the
associative sense.
Deﬁnition 13.2 We say that 퐼is a chiral Poisson ideal of 푉if 푎(푛)퐼⊂퐼for all 푎∈푉,
푛∈Z⩾0.
Thus a Poisson vertex ideal of 푉is a chiral Poisson ideal that is stable under the
action of the translation operator 푇. The quotient space 푉/퐼inherits a Poisson vertex
algebra structure from 푉if 퐼is a Poisson vertex ideal. Note that if 퐼is a vertex
(resp. chiral) Poisson ideal of 푉, then so is its radical
√
퐼([96, §3.3.2]).

since the sum of two chiral Poisson ideals is chiral Poisson. Set

and deﬁne a relation ∼on L by
푥∼푦⇐⇒P푉(픪푥) = P푉(픪푦),

Chiral symplectic cores are expected to be the ﬁnest possible algebraic stratiﬁcation

Chapter 14
Representation theory of W-algebras
Since the Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction applies to any 푉-module for 푉a 푉푘(픤)-vertex
algebra, with 푘∈C, it allows us to consider the associated variety of any 푉-module
(see Theorem 9.11). In particular, from the knowledge of the associated variety of
the simple aﬃne vertex algebra 퐿푘(픤), one can compute the associated variety of
the Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)). This chapter develops several appli-
cations to the representation theory of W-algebras.
Section 14.1 is about the rationality question for W-algebras at admissible levels.
Section 14.2 gathers together a few remarks about the non-admissible levels. Sec-
tion 14.3 covers technics to study the collapsing levels, that is, the level for which
a simple W-algebra is isomorphic to its aﬃne vertex algebra. We mainly focus on
the admissible case while there are at the present time some progresses outside the
admissible case, see for instance [6, 112, 26].
Throughout this chapter, 픤will be a simple Lie algebra, with nilpotent cone N.
The universal W-algebra associated with 픤and 푓∈N at the level 푘∈C is denoted
by W푘(픤, 푓). Unless otherwise speciﬁed, we keep the related notation of Chapter 9.
14.1 Rationality of W-algebras
Recall from Theorem 12.2 (ii) that if 푘is an admissible level for 픤, then there is a
nilpotent orbit O푘of 픤such that 푋퐿푘(픤) = O푘. Then by Theorem 12.9 (ii), the simple
W-algebra W푘(픤, 푓) is lisse if 푓∈O푘.

There was a long-standing conjecture generalizing this observation ([126, 187,

Theorem 14.1 (McRae) Assume that 푘is an admissible level for the simple Lie
algebra 픤, and choose 푓∈O푘so that the simple aﬃne W-algebra W푘(픤, 푓) is lisse.
Then W푘(픤, 푓) is also rational.
In [126, 187], the geometrical condition 푓∈O푘for admissible 푘was stated in a
combinatorial way. Before the general proof of McRae, many important steps was
done toward to Theorem 14.1 by several authors.
• for 픤arbitrary and 푓= 푓reg a regular nilpotent element ([18]),
• for 픤= 픰픩3 and 푓= 푓min a minimal nilpotent element, in which case the
corresponding W-algebra is the Bershadsky–Polyakov vertex algebra ([16]),
• for 픤= 픰픩푛and arbitrary 푓, or 픤of type 퐴, 퐷, 퐸and 푓= 푓subreg a subregular
nilpotent element ([28]),
• for 픤= 픰픩4 and 푓= 푓subreg a subregular nilpotent element ([92]),
• for 픤= 픰픭4 and 푓= 푓subreg a subregular nilpotent element ([110]), in which case
the component group nontrivially acts.

admissible levels
As already indicated, there are lisse and quasi-lisse W-algebras outside the admissi-

In [5] it was shown that collapsing levels have remarkable applications to the
representation theory of aﬃne vertex algebras. They are also useful in elucidating
the structure of modular tensor categories of representations of simple 푊-algebras at
admissible, not necessarily collapsing, levels [28]. Furthermore it has recently been
observed [278] that many collapsing levels for quasi-lisse 푊-algebras should come
from non-trivial isomorphisms of 4D 푁= 2 SUSY ﬁeld theories, via the 4D/2D
duality [57] (see Section 15.3).
There is a full classiﬁcation of collapsing levels for the case that 푓is a minimal
nilpotent element 푓min, including the case in which 픤is a simple Lie superalgebra
([3, 4]). It can be summarized as follows: for 푓= 푓min, 푘is collapsing if and only
if 푘≠−ℎ∨and 푝(푘) = 0, where 푝is a polynomial of degree two with coeﬃcients
in Q.
Example 14.2 If 픤is the Lie superalgebra 픰픩(푚|푛), 푛≠푚, then 푘is collapsing if and
only if (푘+1)(푘+(푚−푛)/2) = 0. (Here, our standard reference for Lie superalgebras
is [177].) If 픤is of type 퐸6, then 푘is collapsing if and only if (푘+ 3)(푘+ 4) = 0, etc.
Furthermore, there isa full classiﬁcation of pairs (픤, 푘) such that W푘(픤, 푓min)  C.
It was obtained by the authors in [40], and then extended to the super case in [5].
For the non super case, the statement is the following.
Theorem 14.4 We have W푘(픤, 푓min)  C if and only if either 픤belongs to the
Deligne exceptional series and 푘= −ℎ∨/6 −1, or 픤= 픰픭2푟, 푟⩾2, 푘= −1/2, or
픤= 픰픩2 and 푘+ 2 = 2/3 or 3/2.
In particular, for 픤= 퐷4, 퐸6, 퐸7, 퐸8 and 푘= −ℎ∨/6 −1, W푘(픤, 푓min)픤♮[[푡]]  C
is lisse. Kawasetsu’s description of the vertex algebra W−ℎ∨/6(픤, 푓min) implies that
W푘+1(픤, 푓min)픤♮[[푡]] is lisse (and rational). See Example 12.4 and Conjecture 12.12
for related topics.
Conjecture 14.5 If W푘(픤, 푓min)픤♮[[푡]] is lisse for some 푘, then W푘+푛(픤, 푓min)픤♮[[푡]]
is lisse for all 푛∈Z⩾0.
For more general nilpotent elements 푓, since the commutation relations in
W푘(픤, 푓) are unknown, the strategy is more complicated. To discover new collaps-
ing levels, more indirect methods were used in [30], and two important invariants of

The following proposition is useful to obtain explicit decompositions of ﬁnite
extensions of admissible simple aﬃne vertex algebras (see [30]).
Proposition 14.2 Let 푘and 푘♮be admissible, 푓∈O푘. Suppose that the associ-
ated varieties of 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) and 퐿푘♮(픤♮) have the same dimension and are not
isomorphic. Then 푘is not collapsing.
Proof The assumption ensures that 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) is nonzero. Hence, W푘(픤, 푓) is
a quotient of 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) and its associated variety is a Zariski closed subvariety
of that of 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)). On the other hand, since 푘is admissible, the associated
variety of 퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) is irreducible, O푘being unibranch (see Appendix D.6.4). If
푘were collapsing, then the associated variety of W푘(픤, 푓) would be isomorphic to the
(irreducible) variety 푋퐿푘♮(픤♮), and so, would have the same dimension as 푋퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤))
by the hypothesis. Then it would be isomorphic to the variety 푋퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)), since
푋퐷푆푓(퐿푘(픤)) is irreducible. This contradicts the non-isomorphism hypothesis.
□

14.3.2 Associated varieties

14.3.3 Main results
Using Theorem 14.6, many new inﬁnite families of (admissible) collapsing levels
for 픤of classical type, and a huge number of (admissible) collapsing levels for 픤
of exceptional type have been detected. Roughly speaking, a collapsing nilpotent
Slodowy slice will yield a collapsing level if the asymptotic data of the vertex
algebras corresponding to the two sides of (14.5) can be shown to coincide. In fact
asymptotic data is rather diﬃcult to compute, so the strategy in many examples is to
ﬁrst compare the central charges.
Assume that the simple Lie algebra 픤is of classical types 픰픩푛, 픰픭푛or 픰픬푛. We
detect collapsing nilpotent Slodowy slices using the row/column removal rule of
Kraft–Procesi as follows. Let 푘be an admissible level for 픤, and let 흀0 the partition
of 푛such that
푋퐿푘(픤) = O푘= O흀0.
We consider all partitions 흁such that O흁⊂O흀0 and, if 흀′
0 and 흁′ denote the
partitions obtained from 흀0 and 흁, respectively, by erasing the common rows and
columns, 흁′ corresponds to a zero nilpotent orbit in the corresponding classical Lie
algebra. In particular the nilpotent Slodowy slice S흀0,흁is collapsing in the notation
(D.5).
The work in the exceptional types is more exhaustive in a sense, since one can
directly exploit the description of the 푘♮
푖and the central charge to detect all possible
collapsing levels. In this way, many nontrivial isomorphisms between a Slodowy
slice SO, 푓and a product of nilpotent orbit closures in 픤♮are obtained. Many of
these were observed in [133] already, though others seem to be new. On the other
hand, of course, many of isomorphisms between Slodowy slices obtained in [133]
do not correspond to collapsing levels.

14.3.4 Related problems and further works
Aside from determination of collapsing levels, the methods exploiting asymptotic
data can be used to prove other results of a similar ﬂavour. For instance, if 픤= 픰픭푛
and 푘= −ℎ∨+ 푝/푞where 푞is twice an odd integer and 푝= ℎ+1, with ℎthe Coxeter
number, then for 푓∈O푘= O흀0 where 흀0 = ( 푞

2 )푚, 2),

We propose the following conjectural extension of Theorem Theorem 14.8.
Conjecture 14.11 Let 푓∈픰픩푛be a nilpotent element associated with a partition
(푞푚, 흂), where 1 < 푚⩽푚0 in the notation of Theorem 14.8, and 흂= (휈1, . . . , 휈푡)
is a partition of 푠:= 푛−푞푚such that 휈1 < 푞. Then
W−푛+푛/푞(픰픩푛, 푓)  W−푠+푠/푞(픰픩푠, 푓′),
where 푓′ is a nilpotent element in 픰픩푠associated with the partition 흂.
Note that Conjecture 14.11 has been proven in the special case where 푛= 7,
푞= 3 and 푠= 4 by Francesco Allegra [8]. This case is in fact a particular case of
Theorem 14.8 used with 푓corresponding to the partitions (32, 1), (3, 22) and (3, 14).
It seems that this conjecture has been stated in [278].
The associated variety of W−푛+푛/푞(픰픩푛, 푓) is S(푞푚0 ,푠0),(푞푚,흂) while the associated
variety of W−푠+푠/푞(픰픩푠, 푓′) is S(푞푚0−푚,푠0),흂. These two nilpotent Slodowy slices are
isomorphic by Lemma D.5.

In another direction, the above methods allow to obtain many cases where
W푘(픤, 푓) is merely a ﬁnite extension of its simple aﬃne vertex algebra 퐿푘♮(픤♮).
Here, by ﬁnite extension we mean here a vertex algebra 푊which decomposes as
a ﬁnite direct sum of irreducible modules over its conformal vertex subalgebra 푉.
For instance, if 픤= 픰픭푛, and if 푓∈O푘is a nilpotent element corresponding to the

Another example is the following for 픤= 퐸6:
퐿−3+4/3(퐴2) ⊗퐿−3+4/3(퐴2) ↩−! W−12+13/3(퐸6, 퐴2).
(14.7)
We refer to [30] for more examples in both classical types and exceptional types.
In the isomorphism (14.6), note that the associated varieties of both sides are iso-
morphic, while it is not the case for the isomorphism (14.7). Indeed, the asso-
ciated variety of W−12+13/2(퐸6, 퐴1) is the nilpotent Slodowy slice2 S2퐴2+퐴1,퐴1
which is not isomorphic to the the product N퐴2 × N퐴2, the associated variety of

morphism.

Chapter 15
4D/2D duality
The vertex algebras (or vertex operator algebras) appear in string theory in physics.
They give the rigorous mathematical deﬁnition of the chiral part of two-dimensional
quantum ﬁeld theories, that were intensively studied since the pioner works of Be-
liakov, Polyakov and Zamolodchikov [62]. By a recent discovery, which goes back
to Nakajima [243] the vertex operator algebras also appear in higher dimensional
quantum ﬁeld theories as well in several ways. This gives a new insights for the rep-
resentation theory of vertex operator algebras. We explore in this chapter interesting
problems that are motivated by results and conjectures in the higher dimensional
quantum ﬁeld theory.
15.1 The Higgs branch conjecture
In the study of four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theories (SCFTs) in
physics, Beem, Lemos, Liendo, Peelaers, Rastelli, and van Rees [57] have constructed
a remarkable map
V: {4D N = 2 SCFTs} −! {vertex operator algebras}
(15.1)
that canonically associates to any four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld
theory T a (two-dimensional) vertex operator algebra V(T) that encodes the spectrum
and the OPE coeﬃcients of the Schur operators. This correspondence between
four-dimensional superconformal ﬁeld theories and two-dimensional vertex operator
algebras, that we refer to as the 4D/2D duality, illuminates both sides.
This map must verify, among other properties, that the character of the vertex
algebra V(T) coincides with the Schur index ISchur(T), which is a formal 푞-series,
of the corresponding four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory T:
ISchur(T) (푞) = 휒V(T) (푞).
(15.2)
According to [57], we have
261

=====PDF PAGE 264=====
262
15 4D/2D duality
푐2퐷(V(T)) = −12푐4퐷(T),
where 푐4퐷(T) and 푐2퐷(V(T)) are the central charges of the four-dimensional N = 2
superconformal ﬁeld theory T and the corresponding vertex algebra V(T), respec-
tively. Since the central charge is positive for a unitary theory, this implies that
the vertex algebras obtained by V are never unitary. In particular, integrable aﬃne
vertex algebras never appear by this correspondence. The main examples of vertex
algebras considered in [57] are the simple aﬃne vertex algebras 퐿푘(픤) of types
퐷4, 퐸6, 퐸7, 퐸8 at level 푘= −ℎ∨
6 −1 that appear in Theorem 12.5, which are non-
