Section 12.3.2). One can ﬁnd more examples in this book (cf. e.g., Section 9.2.3
and Section 9.1.4), see also [58, 78, 91, 79, 279, 264, 77].
There is another important invariant (or, observable) of a four-dimensional
N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory T, called the Higgs branch, which we denote
by Higgs(T). The Higgs branch Higgs(T) is an aﬃne algebraic variety that has the
hyperKähler structure in its smooth part. In particular, Higgs(T) is a (possibly singu-
lar) symplectic variety. Let T be one of the four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal
ﬁeld theorys studied in [57] such that V(T) = 퐿푘(픤) with 푘= −ℎ∨
6 −1 for types 퐷4,
퐸6, 퐸7, 퐸8 as above. It is known that Higgs(T) equals Omin, the minimal nilpotent
orbit of the corresponding simple Lie algebra, and this is equal (cf. Theorem 12.5)
to the associated variety 푋V(T). It is expected that this is not just a coincidence [55].
Conjecture 15.1 For all four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory T, we
have
Higgs(T) = 푋V(T).
15.2 Class S
There is a distinguished class of four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld
theories, called the theory of class S [136, 137]. Class S theories are those that arise
from compactiﬁcation of any of the N = (2, 0) six-dimensional superconformal
theories on a Riemann surface. They are labeled by a complex semisimple group 퐺
and a punctured Riemann surface.
The vertex algebras associated to the theory of class S by the above 4D/2D duality
are called the chiral algebras of class S [58]. In [23], the ﬁrst author constructed
the chiral algebra VS
퐺,푏of class S corresponding to the group 퐺and a 푏-punctured
Riemann surface of genus zero. Chiral algebras of class S associated with higher
genus Riemann surfaces are obtained by glueing such vertex algebras; see [58], or
[267, 268] for mathematical expositions.
According to Moore and Tachikawa [240], the Higgs branches of the theory of
class S associated with such 퐺and 푏are exactly the Moore–Tachikawa symplectic
varieties 푊퐺,푏that have been mathematically constructed by Braverman, Finkelberg
and Nakajima [72] in terms of two-dimensional topological quantum ﬁeld theories.

realized in several ways. Since the map V is well-deﬁned, whenever this happens,
it means that we have an isomorphisms between W-algebras. Such a phenomenon
essentially reﬂects that the level is collapsing, provided that one of the W-algebras
in an aﬃne vertex algebra.
15.4 Other conjectures and open problems
It immediately from [72] and [23] that the Poisson structure of the associated variety
of VS
퐺,푏is symplectic on its smooth locus. The Higgs branch of a four-dimensional
N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory is expected have a ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves
([55]). Hence, it is natural to expect the following.
Conjecture 15.2 The vertex algebra V(T) is quasi-lisse for any four-dimensional
N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory T.
Conjecture 15.2 is consistent with Conjecture 13.3 because the Higgs branch
of a four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory is also expected to be
irreducible. Also, Conjecture 15.2 is consistent with Theorem 13.1. Indeed, in view
of Conjecture 15.1 and (15.2), Theorem 13.1 implies that the Schur index ISchur(T) (푞)
of a four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory satisﬁes a modular linear
diﬀerential equation, which is something that has been conjectured in physics ([55]).
Many four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theories have trivial Higgs
branch. So we cannot expect that the associated variety 푋V(T) is a complete in-
variant of a four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory even assuming
Conjecture 15.1 true.
More recently, Beem and Rastelli conjectured the following [56]:
Conjecture 15.3 Zhu’s 퐶2-algebra 푅V(T), or equivalently ˜푋V(T), is a complete in-
variant of any four-dimensional N = 2 superconformal ﬁeld theory.
Conjecture 15.3 suggests that the vertex operator algebras coming from the 4D/2D
duality are very special. It means that for such vertex algebras 푉, it is expected that
many invariants (e.g., the normalized character) must be recovered from 푅푉. In
particular, we expect that for such vertex operator algebras, the natural surjective

Poisson vertex algebra morphism J∞푅푉! gr퐹푉is an isomorphism, that is,
J∞푅푉 gr퐹푉.
(15.3)
By Corollary 13.1, the isomorphism (15.3) holds for vertex algebras whose associated
variety is smooth, reduced and symplectic. In general, for an arbitrary quasi-lisse
vertex algebra, the isomorphism (15.3) does not hold. Using the chiral homology
of elliptic curves with coeﬃcients in a conformal vertex algebra 푉, Van Ekeren and
Heluani have proved the following surprising result [103].
Theorem 15.4 Let 푉= Vir푝,푞be the Virasoro minimal model with central charge
푐(푝, 푞) := 1 −6 (푝−푞)2

Note that the Virasoro minimal models do not appear as vertex algebras from the

Further, Beem and Rastelli states that vertex operator algebras coming from the
4D/2D duality should carry a ﬁltration, called the 푅-ﬁltration, induced from the
푅-charge of the 4D theory. In general that ﬁltration has not been understood from a
mathematical point of view (see [263] for some partial progress in this direction).

A.5 Integrable and admissible representations
A.5.1 Integrable representations
The representation 퐿(휆), for 휆∈˜픥∗, is ﬁnite dimensional if and only if 휆= 0, that is,

Deﬁnition A.4 A representation 푀of ˜픤is said to be integrable if
(1) 푀is ˜픥-diagonalizable,

휆+ 푚훼푖∉wt(푀) and 휆−푚훼푖∉wt(푀).

A.5.2 Level of a representation
According to the well-known Schur Lemma, any central element of a Lie algebra
acts as a scalar on a simple ﬁnite dimensional representation 퐿. As the Schur Lemma
extends to a representation with countable dimension, the result holds for highest
weight ˜픤-modules. In particular, 퐾∈˜픤acts as a scalar 푘∈C on the simple
representations of the category O.
Deﬁnition A.5 A representation 푀is said to be level 푘if 퐾acts as 푘Id on 푀.
All simple representations of the category O have a level. Namely, 퐿(휆) has level

A.5.3 Admissible representations
We now introduce a class of representations, called admissible representations,
which includes the class of integrable representations. The deﬁnition goes back to
Kac and Wakimoto [185]. While the notion of integrable representations has a geo-
metrical meaning, the notion of admissible representations is purely combinatorial.
However, conjecturally, admissible representations are precisely the representations
which satisfy a certain modular invariant property (see below).

B Poisson algebras, Poisson varieties and Hamiltonian reduction
B.3 Symplectic leaves
If 푋is smooth, then one may view 푋as a complex-analytic manifold equipped with
a holomorphic Poisson structure. For each point 푥∈푋one deﬁnes the symplectic
leaf L푥through 푥to be the set of points that could be reached from 푥by going along
Hamiltonian ﬂows1.
If 푋is not necessarily smooth, let Sing(푋) be the singular locus of 푋, and for any
푘⩾1 deﬁne inductively Sing푘(푋) := Sing(Sing푘−1(푋)). We get a ﬁnite partition
푋= Ã
푘푋푘, where the strata 푋푘:= Sing푘−1(푋) \ Sing푘(푋) are smooth analytic
varieties (by deﬁnition we put 푋0 = 푋\ Sing(푋)). It is known (cf. e.g., [76]) that
each 푋푘inherits a Poisson structure. So for any point 푥∈푋푘there is a well deﬁned
symplectic leaf L푥⊂푋푘. In this way one deﬁnes symplectic leaves on an arbitrary
Poisson variety. In general, each symplectic leaf is a connected smooth analytic (but
not necessarily algebraic) subset in 푋. However, if the algebraic variety 푋consists
of ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves only, then it was shown in [76] that each leaf is
a smooth irreducible locally-closed algebraic subvariety in 푋, and the partition into
symplectic leaves gives an algebraic stratiﬁcation of 푋.
Example B.4 If 픤= Lie(퐺) is an algebraic Lie algebra, the space 픤∗is a (smooth)
Poisson variety and the symplectic leaves of 픤∗are the coadjoint orbits of 픤∗, cf.
[272, Proposition 3.1].
If 픤is simple, the nilpotent cone N of 픤, which is the (reduced) subscheme of 픤∗
associated with the augmentation ideal C[픤∗]퐺
+ of the ring of invariants C[픤∗], is an
example of Poisson variety with ﬁnitely many symplectic leaves. These are precisely
the nilpotent orbits of 픤∗ 픤.

of 푋containing 푥.

Appendix D
Nilpotent orbits of a simple Lie algebra
Let 퐺be a complex connected, simple algebraic group of adjoint type with Lie
algebra 픤, We keep all the related notations used in Appendix A.
D.1 Nilpotent cone
Our main references for the results of this section are [170, 90, 269].
Let N = N(픤) be the nilpotent cone of 픤, that is, the set of all nilpotent elements
of 픤:
N = {푥∈픤: (ad 푥)푚= 0 for some 푚}.
If 픤is a simple Lie algebra of matrices, the nilpotent cone N coincides with the
set of nilpotent matrices of 픤. For 푒∈픤, we denote by 퐺.푒= {(Ad 푔)(푒) : 푔∈퐺}
its adjoint 퐺-orbit. The nilpotent cone is a ﬁnite union of nilpotent 퐺-orbits and it
is itself the closure of the regular nilpotent orbit, denoted by Oreg. It is the unique
nilpotent orbit of codimension the rank 푟of 픤. An element 푥∈픤is regular if its
centralizer 픤푥has the minimal dimension, that is, the rank ℓof 픤. Thus, Oreg is the
set of all regular nilpotent elements of 픤. Regular nilpotent elements are sometimes
called principal and the regular nilpotent orbit the principal nilpotent orbit.
Example D.1 If 픤= 픰픩푛, then the rank of 픤is 푛−1 and Oreg is the conjugacy class
of the 푛-size Jordan block 퐽푛, i.e., Oreg = {푔퐽푛푔−1 : 푔∈SL푛} with

Next, there is a unique dense open orbit in N \ Oreg which is called the subregular
nilpotent orbit of 픤, and denoted by Osubreg. Its codimension in 픤is the rank of
픤plus two. At the extreme opposite, there is a unique nilpotent orbit of smallest
positive dimension called the minimal nilpotent orbit of 픤, and denoted by Omin. Its
dimension is 2(ℎ∨−1) ([276]), where ℎ∨is the dual Coxeter number.
D.2 Chevalley order
The set of nilpotent orbits in 픤is naturally a poset P with partial order ⩽, called the
Chevalley order, or closure order, deﬁned as follows: O′ ⩽O if and only if O′ ⊆O.
If O′ ⊊O we say that O′ is a degeneration of O. The degeneration is called minimal
if O′ is open in O \ O.
The regular nilpotent orbit Oreg is maximal and the zero orbit is minimal with
respect to this order. Moreover, Osubreg is maximal in the poset P \ Oreg and Omin is
minimal in the poset P \ {0}.
The Chevalley order on P corresponds to a partial order on the set P(푛) of
partitions of 푛, for 푛> 1, for 픤= 픰픩푛, ﬁrst described by Gerstenhaber. More generally,
the Chevalley order corresponds to a partial order on some subset of P(푛) when 픤
is of classical type as we explain below.
The notions of degeneration and minimal degeneration can be expressed in term
of partitions.
Deﬁnition D.1 Let 흀∈P(푛). A degeneration of 흀is an element 흁∈P(푛) such
that O흁< O흀, that is, 흁< 흀. A degeneration 흁of 흀is said to be minimal if O흁is
open in O흀\ O흀.
Let 푛∈Z>0. As a rule, unless otherwise speciﬁed, we write an element 흀of
P(푛) as a decreasing sequence 흀= (휆1, . . . , 휆푠) omitting the zeroes. Thus,

An important consequence of Lemma D.3 is that there are only ﬁnitely many
nilpotent orbits, namely at most 3rank픤. Also, the weighted Dynkin diagram is a
complete invariant, i.e., two such diagrams are equal if and only if the corresponding
nilpotent orbits are equal, [90, Theorem 3.5.4].
The regular nilpotent orbit always corresponds to the weighted Dynkin diagram
with only 2’s (this result is not obvious, cf. e.g., [90, Theorem 4.1.6] for a proof).
More generally, a nilpotent orbit is even if and only if the weighted Dynkin diagram
have only 2’s or 0’s.
D.4 The nilpotent cone
The nilpotent cone enjoys remarkable properties. We collect some of them in this
section, omitting the proofs.
Let 픥be a Cartan subalgebra of 픤, and denote by 푊the Weyl group of (픤, 픥). The
restriction map C[픤] ! C[픥] induces an algebra isomorphism C[픤]퐺 C[픥]푊,
referred to as the Chevalley isomorphism. As a consequence, the algebra C[픤]퐺is a
is a polynomial algebra of dimension ℓ, the rank of 픤(which is the dimension of 픥),
since 푊is a ﬁnite group.
The cone nilpotent turns out to be the zero locus of the augmentation ideal C[픤]퐺
+
of C[픤]퐺, that is, the zero locus of homogeneous generators 푝1, . . . , 푝푟of C[픤]퐺:

+ } = Spec C[픤]/(푝1, . . . , 푝ℓ).
(D.2)
Theorem D.3 Let N be the nilpotent cone of 픤.
(i)
The scheme N is reduced, irreductible and it is a complete intersection.
(ii)
The scheme N has rational singularities. Namely, there exists a proper bira-
tional morphism 휏: 푌! N such that Y is smooth over C, 휏∗(O푌) = ON and
푅푝휏∗(O푌) = 0 for all 푝⩾1.

D.5 Associated varieties of primitive ideals
Let 퐼be a two-sided ideal of the enveloping algebra 푈(픤). The PBW ﬁltration on
푈(픤) induces a ﬁltration on 퐼, so that gr 퐼becomes a graded Poisson ideal in C[픤∗]
(see Example B.2). Denote by V(퐼) the zero locus of gr 퐼in 픤∗,
V(퐼) := {휆∈픤∗: 푝(휆) = 0 for all 푝∈gr 퐼} = Specm (C[픤∗]/gr 퐼).
The set V(퐼) is usually referred to as the associated variety of 퐼. Identifying 픤∗
with 픤through a nondegenerate bilinear symmetric form on 픤, we shall often view
associated varieties of two-sided ideals of 푈(픤) as subsets of 픤.
A proper two-sided ideal 퐼of푈(픤) is called primitive if it is the annihilator in푈(픤)
of a simple left푈(픤)-module. There are two fundamental results ([101, 68, 201, 173])
on primitive ideals of 푈(픤).
Theorem D.4 (Duﬂo) Any primitive ideal in 푈(픤) is the annihilator Ann푈(픤) 퐿픤(휆)
in 푈(픤) of some irreducible highest weight representation 퐿픤(휆) of 픤, where 휆∈픥∗.
Theorem D.5 (Joseph) Let 퐼be a primitive ideal of 푈(픤).
(i)
The associated variety V(퐼) is contained in the nilpotent cone N of 픤.
(ii)
The associated variety V(퐼) is irreducible and, hence, it is the closure O of
some nilpotent orbit O in 픤.
Part (i) of the theorem is not hard to prove. By Theorem D.4, there is 휆∈픥∗such
that 퐼= Ann푈(픤) 퐿픤(휆). Consider the central character 휒휆: 푍(픤) ! C, where 푍(픤)
is the center of 푈(픤), associated to 휆deﬁned by (A.14). Its kernel is a maximal ideal
of 푍(픤) contained in Ann푈(픤) 퐿픤(휆). We deduce that gr 퐼contains the symbols of all
elements in ker 휒휆. Since gr 푍(픤) = 푆(픤)픤, we easily see that these elements generate
the augmentation ideal 푆(픤)픤
+. As a consequence, gr 퐼⊃푆(픤)픤
+, whence V(퐼) ⊂N
by (D.2).
Part (ii) is much harder to establish. Theorem D.5 (ii) was ﬁrst partially proved (by
a case-by-case argument) in [68], and in a more conceptual way in [201] and [173]
(independently), using many earlier deep results due to Joseph, Gabber, Lusztig,
Vogan and others.
Diﬀerent primitive ideals may share the same associated variety. At the same
time, not all nilpotent orbit closures appear as associated variety of some primitive
ideal of 푈(픤).
Let 휆∈픥∗, and set
퐽휆:= Ann푈(픤) (퐿픤(휆)).

V(퐽휆) = O휆

D.6.1 Smoothly equivalent singularities
Consider two varieties 푋,푌and two points 푥∈푋, 푦∈푌. We refer to [161] for the

Deﬁnition D.3 The singularity of 푋at 푥is called smoothly equivalent to the sin-

D.6.3 Minimal degenerations

D.6.4 Branching
Let 푋be an irreducible algebraic variety, and 푥∈푋. We say that 푋is unibranch
at 푥if the normalization 휋: ( ˜푋, 푥) ! (푋, 푥) of (푋, 푥) is locally a homeomorphism
at 푥([153, Chapter III, §4.3]; see also [133, §2.4]). Otherwise, we say that 푋has
branches at 푥and the number of branches of 푋at 푥is the number of connected
components of 휋−1(푥) [63, §5,(E)].
As it is explained in [133, §2.4], the number of irreducible components of SO, 푓
is equal to the number of branches of O at 푓. If an irreducible algebraic variety 푋is
normal, then it is obviously unibranch at any point 푥∈푋. The converse is not true.
For instance, there is no branching in type 퐺2 but one knows that the nilpotent orbit
˜퐴1 of 퐺2 of dimension 8 is not normal [200].
The number of branches of O at 푓, and so the number of irreducible components
of SO, 푓, can be determined from the tables of Green functions in [258], as discussed
in [63, Section 5,(E)–(F)]. We indicate in Table D.1 the nilpotent orbits O which
have branchings in types 퐹4, 퐸6, 퐸7 and 퐸8 (there is no branching in type 퐺2). The

D.6.5 Normality of nilpotent orbit closures in the classical types
The normality question of nilpotent orbit closures in the classical types is completely
answered ([161, 197, 199, 262]). First of all, if 픤= 픰픩푛, then all nilpotent orbit closures
are normal ([197]). To explain the results in the other types, we now focus on the
orthogonal and symplectic Lie algebras. We assume in the rest of the section that 픤
is either the Lie algebra 픬푛of the orthogonal group 푂(푛), or the Lie algebra 픰픬푛of
the special orthogonal group 푆푂(푛), or the Lie algebra 픰픭푛of the symplectic group
푆푃(푛).
The following result is due to Kraft and Procesi ([199, Theorem 1]).
Theorem D.6 Let O be a nilpotent orbit in 픬푛or 픰픭푛.
(i)
O is normal if and only if it is unibranch.
(ii)
O is normal if and only if it is normal in codimension 2.
In particular, O is normal if it does not contain a nilpotent orbit O′ ⩽O of codi-
mension 2. Theorem D.6 does not hold if 픤= 픰픬2푛and if O = O1,흀, with 흀very
even.
By Theorem D.6, for the normality question, it is enough to consider minimal
휀-degeneration of codimension 2 (except for the very even nilpotent orbits in 픰픬2푛).
In the setting of Lemma D.6 we say that the 휀-degeneration 휼⩽흀is obtained from
the 휀′-degeneration 휼′ ⩽흀′ by adding rows and columns. An 휀-degeneration 휼⩽흀
is called irreducible if it cannot be obtained by adding rows and columns in a non
trivial way.
When we obtain an irreducible pair (휼′, 흀′), such a pair is called the type of
(O휀,휼, O휀,흀).

Appendix E
Superalgebras and Cliﬀord algebras
A superspace is a C-vector space 퐸equipped with a Z2-grading, 퐸= 퐸¯0 ⊕퐸¯1.
Elements in 퐸¯0 are called even, elements of 퐸¯1 are called odd. We denote by
|푣| ∈{¯0, ¯1} the parity of homogeneous elements 푣∈퐸. A morphism of superspaces
is a linear map preserving Z2-gradings. It is itself a superspace by:
Hom(퐸, 퐹) ¯0 = Hom(퐸¯0, 퐹¯0) ⊕Hom(퐸¯1, 퐹¯1),
Hom(퐸, 퐹) ¯1 = Hom(퐸¯0, 퐹¯1) ⊕Hom(퐸¯1, 퐹¯0).
The category of superspaces is a tensor category. Then one may deﬁne superalgebras,
Lie superalgebras, Poisson superalgebras, etc. as the algebra objects, Lie algebra
objects, Poisson algebra objects etc. in this tensor category.
E.1 Lie superalgebras, Poisson superalgebras and tensor product
For example, a Lie superalgebra is a superspace 퐴together with a bracket [−, −] : 퐴×
퐴! 퐴such that for all homogeneous elements 푎, 푏∈퐴,
[푎, 푏] = −(−1) |푎||푏|[푏, 푎],
[[푎, 푏], 푐] = [푎, [푏, 푐]] −(−1) |푎||푏|[푏, [푎, 푐]].
Note that any superalgebra 퐴is naturally a Lie superalgebra by setting for all
homogeneous elements 푎, 푏∈퐴,
[푎, 푏] = 푎푏−(−1) |푎||푏|푏푎.
It is supercommutative if [퐴, 퐴] = 0.
A superspace 퐴is a Poisson superalgebra if it is equipped with a bracket
{−, −}: 퐴× 퐴! 퐴such that (퐴, {−, −}) is a Lie superalgebra and for any 푎∈퐴,
the operator {푎, −}: 퐴! 퐴is a superderivation: for all homogeneous elements
